L9: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
L10: **Disclaimer:** FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project Orbis. FDA collaborated with Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency (ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Israel's Ministry of Health (IMoH), and United Kingdom's Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect FDA's completed review of the application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.
L11: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant, which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L12: Application Type	BLA
Application Number(s)	BLA 761344
Priority or Standard	Priority
Submit Date(s)	October 12, 2023
Received Date(s)	October 12, 2023
PDUFA Goal Date	June 12, 2024
Division/Office	Division of Oncology 2/ Office of Oncologic Diseases
Review Completion Date	May 16, 2024
Established Name	Tarlatamab
(Proposed) Trade Name	IMDELLTRA
Pharmacologic Class	Delta-like Ligand 3-directed CD3 T-cell Engager
Code name	AMG 757
Applicant	Amgen, Inc. (Amgen)
Formulation(s)	Intravenous
Dosing Regimen	An initial dose of 1 mg on Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter
L13: Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	IMDELLTRA is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with advanced small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Accelerated Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	IMDELLTRA is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy

12.3	Pharmacokinetics	291
Effects of Tarlatamab on CYP450 Substrates	292
Tarlatamab causes transient release of cytokines that may suppress CYP450 enzymes and may result in an increased exposure of concomitant CYP substrates during and up to 14 days after occurrence of cytokine release syndrome [see Clinical Pharmacology (12.2)].	292

L48: ## Glossary
L49: ADA	antidrug antibodies
ADME	absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion
AIC	akaike information criterion
ALT	alanine aminotransferase
AMQ	Amgen MedDRA query
AST	aspartate aminotransferase
AUC	area under the concentration-time curve
AUCLast	area under the curve from time of dosing to the last measurable concentration
BIC	bayesian information criterion
BICR	blinded independent central review
BiTE	bispecific T-cell engager
BLA	Biologics License Application
BOR	best overall response
BSC	best supportive care
BTSR	best tumor size response

C1q	complement component 1q

CKD	chronic kidney disease
CL	clearance
Cmax	maximum observed concentration
COA	Clinical Outcome Assessment
CR	complete response
CSF	cerebral spinal fluid
CRP	C-reactive protein
CRS	cytokine release syndrome
CSR	clinical study report
CTCAE	common terminology criteria for adverse events
DCO	data cutoff
DCR	disease control rate
DLL3	delta-like ligand 3
DoR	duration of response
ECG	electrocardiogram
ECOG	Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group
EDC	electronic data capture
eCRF	electronic case report form
L50: eCTD	electronic common technical document
EMR	electronic medical record
EOI	events of interest

Fc\gammaR	Fc-gamma receptors
FcRn	neonatal Fc receptor complex
FDA	Food and Drug Administration
FIH	first-in-human
GCP	good clinical practice
G-CSF	granulocyte-colony stimulating factor
GD	gestation days
GT	grouped term
GLP	good laboratory practice
ICANS	immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome
ICH	International Council for Harmonisation
IFN	interferon
IFN-\gamma	interferon gamma
IL	interleukin
INN	International Nonproprietary Name
IPD	important protocol deviation

L52: ## 1 Executive Summary
L53: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L54: Tarlatamab-dlle (herein referred to as tarlatamab) is a bispecific T-cell engager that binds to delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) expressed on the surface of cells, including tumor cells, and CD3 expressed on the surface of T-cells. Tarlatamab causes T-cell activation, release of inflammatory cytokines and lysis of DLL3-expressing cells. Tarlatamab is a new molecular entity and is not currently approved in the United States or elsewhere.
L55: In this biologics licensing application (BLA), Amgen's proposed indication for tarlatamab is "for the treatment of adult patients with advanced small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy."
L56: The recommended dosing regimen for the proposed indication is an initial dose of tarlatamab of 1 mg on Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8 and 15 and every 2 weeks thereafter.
L57: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L58: The FDA review teams recommend accelerated approval of tarlatamab for the following indication: *Tarlatamab is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.*
L59: The recommendation for accelerated approval, according to 21 CFR 314.510 Subpart H, is based on the results from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLphi-301), an ongoing, multicenter, open-label, multi-cohort trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of tarlatamab as a single agent in adult patients with relapsed/refractory SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy. The assessment of safety was also supported by results from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and from Study 20160323, an ongoing first-in-human (FIH), open-label, ascending, multiple dose study of tarlatamab in adult patients with advanced SCLC who have progressed or recurred after at least one platinum-based regimen.
L60: The primary efficacy population consists of 99 patients enrolled in Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 who received tarlatamab at the dosage proposed for marketing (initial dose of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter). Of the 99 patients, 73 patients (74%) had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy; of these 73 patients, 58 (59%) received anti-PD-(L)1 therapy in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in the
L61: first line setting. The confirmed blinded independent central review (BICR)-assessed overall response rate (ORR), as per Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors version 1.1 (RECIST v1.1), was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%). The median duration of response (DoR) was 9.7 months (range 2.7 to 20.7+ months); 68% of responders had an observed DoR \ge6 months and 40% of responders had an observed DoR \ge12 months.
L62: FDA considers response rate, supported by DoR, an appropriate early clinical endpoint for SCLC because it is a direct measure of the intervention; in the absence of therapy, tumors do not typically regress on their own. The observed ORR and DoR were clinically meaningful, and responses were observed across subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive and platinum-refractory disease and across subgroups with various tumor DLL3 expression cut-offs.
L63: Cytotoxic chemotherapies administered after disease has progressed on platinum-based chemotherapy are expected to have differential tumor response based on a patient's "sensitivity" to platinum-based chemotherapy, with very low response rates reported for patients with platinum resistant SCLC. Given this, efficacy has typically been assessed separately in subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive or platinum-resistant SCLC; however, given the known mechanism of action of tarlatamab as a targeted agent that causes activation of the immune system, which differs from cytotoxic chemotherapy, platinum sensitivity is not expected to be a predictive factor for response to tarlatamab. With this caveat, it was still considered appropriate to evaluate ORR in these subgroups.
L64: Among the 99 patients who received tarlatamab at the proposed dosage in Study 20200491, 69 patients had available data regarding platinum sensitivity status. In this subgroup of patients, ORR was 31% (95% CI: 18%, 47%) in 42 patients with platinum-sensitive SCLC (defined as \ge90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression) and 52% (95% CI 32%, 71%) in 27 patients with platinum-resistant SCLC (defined as &lt; 90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression). Although the ORR point estimates differ between these subgroups, the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in efficacy based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely with tarlatamab.
L65: For this application, the proposed indication, SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy, differs from the population enrolled in Study 20200491, patients with SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy. Overall, the ORR and DoR data from the primary efficacy population in Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated patient population (3rd line or greater), is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with topotecan, the only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with SCLC previously treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum-sensitive disease and 2-7% in platinum-resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in the primary efficacy population from Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I
L66: inhibitor as second-line therapy. Of the remaining patients, the grand majority (89%) received other second line therapies considered treatment options per U.S. standard of care. The ORR in the subgroups of patients who received prior topoisomerase I inhibitor and who did not receive prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (including and not including patients who received standard of care therapies) were generally consistent across subgroups and consistent with the ORR observed in the overall study population. Furthermore, it is not expected that treatment with additional therapies following platinum-based chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of response to tarlatamab relative to treatment with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy following platinum-based chemotherapy. In light of the observed results and particularly considering the ORR observed with tarlatamab in patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which far exceeds ORR reported with standard of care therapies, the available data for tarlatamab supports the proposed indication for treatment of SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
L67: Overall, the ORR and durable responses observed with tarlatamab in Study 20200491 are consistent with a meaningful advantage over available therapies for the proposed indication and provide substantial evidence of effectiveness and support a recommendation of accelerated approval for tarlatamab for the following indication: *treatment of adult patients with extensive-stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy*.
L68: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L69: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L70: Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related mortality in the US and worldwide (Siegel, 2023). Approximately 15% of lung cancers diagnosed are small cell lung cancer (American Cancer Society, 2024). The majority of patients with SCLC have extensive-stage SCLC (ES-SCLC) at presentation. Current first-line treatment for ES-SCLC generally consists of etoposide in combination with platinum chemotherapy and a programmed death ligand 1 (PD-L1) immune checkpoint inhibitor (atezolizumab or durvalumab). Although the response rate to initial treatment is high with ORR of 60-70%, nearly all patients relapse after initial therapy and the median overall survival from front-line ES-SCLC trials is approximately 12 to 13 months (Liu 2021, Paz-Ares 2022). The 5-year survival for ES-SCLC is less than 2% (Basumallik 2023). Depending on the interval between completion of chemotherapy and detection of relapse, treatment options include retreatment with a platinum-based regimen or use of single agent chemotherapy.
L71: Topotecan is the only therapy with traditional approval in the US for patients with SCLC whose disease has progressed after first-line chemotherapy; the approved indication is for platinum-sensitive SCLC, with topotecan demonstrating an overall response rate (ORR) of 24% (95% CI: 16%, 32%) with a median duration of response (DoR) of 3.3 months. There is no therapy with traditional approval for patients with platinum-resistant SCLC. Lurbinectedin has received accelerated approval for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic SCLC whose disease has progressed on or after platinum-based chemotherapy based on an ORR as assessed by independent review committee of 30% (95% CI: 22%, 40%) with a median DoR of 5.1 months (95% CI: 4.9, 6.4). The ORR was 43% (95% CI 31%, 57%) in the platinum-sensitive subgroup and 13% (95% CI 5%, 27%) in the platinum-resistant subgroup. In addition to topotecan and lurbinectedin, other therapeutic options that are not FDA approved include taxanes, irinotecan, doxorubicin, gemcitabine, and temozolomide.
L72: Tarlatamab is a bispecific delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3)-directed CD3 T-cell engager. Tarlatamab causes T-cell activation, production of inflammatory cytokines and release of cytotoxic proteins which results in redirected lysis of DLL3-expressing cells. Currently, tarlatamab is not approved in the US for any indication.
L73: The evaluation of the efficacy of tarlatamab is based on results from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLLphi-301 trial), an ongoing, multicenter, open-label, multi-cohort trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of tarlatamab as a single agent in adult patients with
L74: relapsed/refractory SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy. The assessment of safety was also supported by results from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and from Study 20160323, an ongoing first-in-human (FIH), open-label, ascending, multiple dose study of tarlatamab in adult patients with advanced SCLC who have progressed or recurred after at least one platinum-based regimen.
L75: The primary efficacy population consists of 99 patients enrolled in Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 who received tarlatamab at the dosage proposed for marketing (initial dose of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter). Of the 99 patients, 73 patients (74%) had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy. Of these 73 patients, 58 (59%) received anti-PD-(L)1 therapy in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in the first line setting. The confirmed blinded independent central review (BICR)-assessed ORR, as per Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors version 1.1 (RECIST v1.1), was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%). The median duration of response (DoR) was 9.7 months (range 2.7 to 20.7+ months); 68% of responders had an observed DoR \ge6 months and 40% of responders had an observed DoR \ge12 months. The observed ORR and DoR were clinically meaningful, and responses were observed across subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive and platinum-refractory disease and across subgroups with various DLL3 expression cut-offs.
L76: Cytotoxic chemotherapies administered after SCLC has progressed on platinum-based chemotherapy are expected to have differential tumor response based on a patient's "sensitivity" to platinum-based chemotherapy, with very low response rates reported for patients with platinum-resistant SCLC. Given this, efficacy has typically been assessed separately in subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive or platinum-resistant SCLC; however, given the known mechanism of action of tarlatamab as a targeted agent that causes activation of the immune system, which differs from cytotoxic chemotherapy, platinum sensitivity is not expected to be a predictive factor for response to tarlatamab. With this caveat, it was still considered appropriate to evaluate ORR in these subgroups.
L77: Among the 99 patients who received tarlatamab at the proposed dosage in Study 20200491, 69 patients had available data regarding platinum sensitivity status. In this subgroup of patients, ORR was 31% (95% CI: 18%, 47%) in 42 patients with platinum-sensitive SCLC (defined as \ge90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression) and 52% (95% CI 32%, 71%) in 27 patients with platinum-resistant SCLC (defined as &lt; 90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression). Although the ORR point estimates differ between these subgroups, the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in efficacy based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely with tarlatamab.
L78: For this application, the proposed indication, SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy, differs from the population enrolled in Study 20200491, patients with SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy. Overall, the ORR and DoR data from Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated patient population (3rd line or greater), is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with topotecan, the only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with SCLC previously treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum-sensitive disease and 2-7% in platinum-resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in of the primary efficacy population from Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I inhibitor as second line therapy. Of the remaining patients, the grand majority (89%) received second line therapies considered treatment options per U.S. standard of care. The ORR in the subgroups of patients who received prior topoisomerase I inhibitor and who did not receive prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (including and not including patients who received standard of care therapies), were generally consistent with the response rate observed for the overall study population. Furthermore, it is not expected that treatment with additional therapies following platinum-based chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of response to tarlatamab relative to treatment with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy following platinum-based chemotherapy. In light of the observed results and particularly considering the ORR observed with tarlatamab in patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which far exceeds ORR reported with standard of care therapies, the available data for tarlatamab supports the proposed indication for treatment of SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
L79: Of note, the primary efficacy population included no Black/African American patients and one Hispanic/Latino patient, which is an underrepresentation of the incidence of SCLC in these racial and ethnic minority groups. Given this, a postmarketing commitment (PMC) will be issued to provide further analyses from ongoing or planned clinical trials to further characterize the safety, efficacy and pharmacokinetics of tarlatamab in these patient populations.
L80: The safety assessment for tarlatamab is primarily supported by a pooled safety population which included 187 patients from Study 20200491 and Study 20160323 with ES-SCLC who were treated with tarlatamab at the dosage proposed for marketing. The most common adverse reactions (\ge 20\%) were cytokine release syndrome, fatigue, pyrexia, dysgeusia, decreased appetite, musculoskeletal pain, constipation, anemia, and nausea.
L81: The key safety concerns for tarlatamab are cytokine release syndrome (CRS) and neurologic toxicity, including immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS), both of which will be included in a Boxed Warning in the USPI. Other safety concerns include hepatotoxicity, infections, cytopenias, and hypersensitivity. These safety concerns are addressed in the Warnings and Precautions and Dosage and
L82: Administration sections of the tarlatamab U.S. Prescribing Information (USPI), and additional mitigation strategies for CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS, are discussed below. The observed safety profile is considered acceptable when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease.
L83: In the pooled safety population (n = 187), CRS occurred in 55% of patients treated with tarlatamab, including 34% Grade 1, 19% Grade 2, 1.1% Grade 3 and 0.5% Grade 4. Recurrent CRS occurred in 24% of tarlatamab-treated patients (18% Grade 1 and 6% Grade 2). Most events (43%) of CRS occurred after the first dose with 29% of patients experiencing any grade CRS after the second dose and 9% of patients experiencing CRS following the third dose or later. Following the Day 1, Day 8, Day 15 infusions, 16%, 4.3%, and 2.1% of patients experienced \ge Grade 2 CRS, respectively. The median time to onset of all grade CRS from most recent dose of tarlatamab was 13.5 hours (range: 1 to 268 hours). The median time to onset of \ge Grade 2 CRS from most recent dose of tarlatamab was 14.6 hours (range: 2 to 566 hours).
L84: Neurologic toxicity including ICANS occurred in 47% of patients in the pooled safety population, including 10% Grade 3; no Grade 4 events were reported. The most frequent neurologic toxicities were headache (14%), peripheral neuropathy (7%), dizziness (7%), insomnia (6%), muscular weakness (3.7%), delirium (2.1%), syncope (1.6%) and neurotoxicity (1.1%). ICANS occurred in 9% of patients and recurrent ICANS occurred in 1.6% of patients. Most patients experienced ICANS following cycle 2 day 1 (24%). Following Day 1, Day 8, and Day 15 infusions, 0.5%, 0.5% and 3.7% of patients experienced \ge Grade 2 ICANS, respectively. The median time to onset of ICANS from the first dose of tarlatamab was 29.5 days (range: 1 to 154 days). The median time to resolution of ICANS was 33 days (range: 1 to 93 days).
L85: Per the protocol for Study 20200491, patients were hospitalized for 48 hours post tarlatamab infusion for Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 doses, except for patients in Part 3 of Study 20200491, who were hospitalized for 24 hours post tarlatamab infusion for Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 doses. Based on review of all currently available safety data, it was determined that monitoring of patients for 22 to 24 hours on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 in an appropriate healthcare setting is acceptable to ensure safe use, and this recommendation is incorporated in labeling.
L86: Based on our review, we have determined that the safety risks of tarlatamab can be adequately conveyed through labeling. The USPI will include Boxed Warnings to communicate the risks of CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS. In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks. A post-marketing requirement (PMR) will also be issued to conduct an integrated safety analysis of data from patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer to further characterize the long-term incidence, severity, and outcome of the known serious risks of cytokine release syndrome, immune effector cell-associated
L87: neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity. This postmarketing requirement is intended to provide additional safety information that is needed to support the safety of the product.
L88: Overall, the ORR and durable responses observed with tarlatamab in Study 20200491 are consistent with a meaningful advantage over available therapies for the proposed indication. Tarlatamab has an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of this life-threatening disease and current available therapy. Based on these results, tarlatamab has a favorable risk-benefit profile in the context of a life-threatening disease with an unmet medical need, and the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. The review team's regulatory recommendation is to grant tarlatamab accelerated approval for the following indication: *Treatment of adult patients with extensive-stage small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.*
L89: Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial, conducted as a PMR. The Applicant proposes to verify the clinical benefit of tarlatamab in the treatment of patients with SCLC who have disease progression on or after prior platinum-containing therapy, based on the results of an ongoing randomized, controlled trial, DeLlphi-304 (Study 20210004), comparing tarlatamab to investigator's choice standard of care therapy with a primary endpoint of overall survival (OS). The status, including current enrollment and appropriateness of the proposed completion date, of the post-marketing trial, Dellphi-304, was assessed during the review of this application and the trial was considered to be well underway.
L90: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related mortality in the US and worldwide. Approximately 15% of lung cancers diagnosed are small cell lung cancer (SCLC). The majority of patients with SCLC have ES-SCLC at presentation.
Current first-line treatment for ES-SCLC is etoposide in combination with platinum chemotherapy and PD-L1 inhibitor therapy (atezolizumab or durvalumab) which is associated with a median overall survival of approximately 9-13 months.
ES-SCLC is a serious and life-threatening condition with poor survival.
L91: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Depending on the interval between completion of chemotherapy and detection of progression or recurrence, treatment options include retreatment with a platinum-based regimen (for patients with recurrence >6 months from last dose of platinum) or use of single-agent chemotherapy

Current Treatment Options	In the second-line setting, topotecan is the only available therapy with traditional FDA approval for patients with SCLC platinum-sensitive disease who have progressed at least 60 days after initiation of first-line chemotherapy; the approved indication is for platinum-sensitive SCLC with topotecan demonstrating an ORR of 24% (95% CI: 16%, 32%) with a median duration of response of 3.3 months
Based on data from single arm studies using the more standard cut-off of ≥90 days for platinum-sensitive vs. platinum-resistant, reported response rates ranged from 11-31% for patients with platinum-sensitive disease and 2-7% in patients with platinum-resistant disease
In addition, lurbinectedin has received accelerated approval for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic SCLC whose disease has progressed on or after platinum-based chemotherapy. In this setting, lurbinectedin demonstrated ORR as assessed by independent review committee of 30% (95% CI: 22%, 40%) with a median DoR of 5.1 months (95% CI: 4.9, 6.4) and 35% of patients having a DoR of ≥6 months. L102: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L103: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L104: ## The Applicant's Position:
L105: Globally, lung cancer is the second most commonly diagnosed cancer and leading cause of cancer death, representing approximately 1 in 10 (11.4%) cancers diagnosed and 1 in 5 (18.0%) cancer deaths (Sung et al, 2021). Small cell lung cancer (SCLC) accounts for approximately 15% of overall lung cancer cases with approximately 330000 new cases worldwide each year based on an estimated 2.2 million cases of lung cancer in 2020 (National Cancer Institute [NCI], 2023; National Organization for Rare Disorders, 2021; Rudin et al, 2021; Sung et al, 2021). There are approximately 270000 new deaths worldwide due to SCLC based on approximately 1.8 million lung cancer deaths in 2020 (Sung et al, 2021).
L106: Small cell lung cancer is a high-grade neuroendocrine tumor marked by an exceptionally high proliferative rate, strong predilection for early metastasis, and poor prognosis (Rudin et al, 2021). While 30% of patients present with disease that can be encompassed by one radiotherapy field (limited stage), the majority of cases have disease diagnosed as extensive stage. Although 20% to 30% of patients with limited-stage SCLC can be cured with radio-chemotherapy, treatment is rarely curative in extensive stage, and SCLC is associated with poor long-term survival (5-year survival is &lt;10%) (American Cancer Society, 2022).
L107: (b) (4)
L108: Overall, the results from the (b) (4) published literature show that patients with SCLC have poor treatment outcomes with existing therapies in second line or later, and their prognosis in the overall population and in the subset treated with first-line anti-PD-L1 therapy is poor. Therefore, there remains a significant unmet medical need in patients with advanced SCLC for novel therapies that can support longer survival and reduce disease-associated symptoms with an acceptable safety profile. Tarlatamab is being developed with the goal of addressing this unmet need.
L109: The FDA's Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant's position.
L110: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L111: ## The Applicant's Position:
L112: Current first-line treatment of SCLC generally consists of etoposide in combination with either cisplatin or carboplatin with the addition of radiation for limited-stage SCLC and the addition of a PD-L1 immune checkpoint inhibitor (atezolizumab or durvalumab) for extensive-stage SCLC (National Comprehensive Cancer Network [NCCN] SCLC Guidelines, 2023; Guidelines for Diagnosis and Treatment of Lung Cancer in Japan, 2022; Rudin et al, 2021; Ganti et al, 2021). Although this first-line regimen in extensive-stage SCLC has a relatively high response rate of 60% to 70%, resistance and relapse almost always occur, usually within the first year after treatment for nearly all patients with extensive-stage SCLC (Rudin et al, 2021; Yoon et al, 2019; Chan and Coward, 2013), and median OS is approximately 12 to 13 months (Tariq et al, 2021; Paz-Ares et al, 2019; Horn et al, 2018).
L113: Treatment options for patients with relapsed SCLC are limited, and with currently available therapy, prognosis remains poor. In addition to consideration of platinum rechallenge or clinical trial participation, other recommended regimens in the relapsed or refractory setting include topotecan (Eckardt et al, 2007; O'Brien et al, 2006; von Pawel et al, 1999), lurbinectedin (Trigo et al, 2020), other chemotherapy agents (eg, cyclophosphamide/doxorubicin/vincristine, docetaxel, etoposide, gemcitabine, temozolomide), or immunotherapy agents (eg, nivolumab, pembrolizumab).
L114: National Comprehensive Cancer Network guidelines include several therapies as other recommended regimens for patients who progress subsequent to initial systemic therapy; however, only topotecan is Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved for use in patients with SCLC platinum-sensitive disease who progress at least 60 days after initiation of first-line chemotherapy (NCCN, 2023) (Table 1). Of note, topotecan was approved based on a study comparing topotecan to cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, and vincristine combination
L115: chemotherapy, which did not show statistically significant improvements in response rate, duration of response (DOR), time to progression, or OS (topotecan US Prescribing Information, 2019; von Pawel et al, 1999). In the study, topotecan demonstrated an objective response rate (ORR) (95% CI) of 24.3% (16.2%, 32.4%) and median (95% CI) DOR of 3.3 (3.0, 4.1) months (Table 1); cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, and vincristine combination chemotherapy demonstrated an ORR (95% CI) of 18.3% (10.8%, 25.7%) and median (95% CI) DOR of 3.5 (3.0, 5.3) months.
L116: National Comprehensive Cancer Network guidelines (2023) also include lurbinectedin as a recommended regimen in patients who progress subsequent to initial systemic therapy with platinum-based chemotherapy (NCCN, 2023). Lurbinectedin received accelerated approval in August 2020 based on an ORR (95% CI) as assessed by investigator of 35% (26%, 45%) and median (95% CI) DOR of 5.3 (4.1, 6.4) months (Zepzelca US Prescribing Information, 2023). It is important to note that all subjects enrolled in this study had progressed on or after platinum-based chemotherapy, with only 8% of subjects also having prior immunotherapy.
L117: Recent novel therapeutic strategies such as immunotherapy with programmed cell death 1 (PD-1) checkpoint inhibitors have not improved outcomes compared with topotecan in the second-line or later setting (Das et al, 2021; Giffin et al, 2021; Trigo et al, 2020), and the need for durable responses remains high. Additionally, the benefits of immunotherapy in first-line treatment of extensive-stage SCLC are relatively modest (approximately 2 to 3 month increase in median OS) (Paz-Ares et al, 2019; Horn et al, 2018) compared with that observed in non-small cell lung cancer in first-line treatment. Small cell lung cancer is considered an immune-cold lung cancer subtype due to the limited infiltration of immune cells, reduced PD-L1 expression, and lack of antigen presentation (Caliman et al, 2023; Sutherland et al, 2022). Although SCLC has a high tumor mutational burden related to the carcinogenic activity of tobacco exposure, this does not appear to translate to a similar favorable response to PD-L1 and PD-1 inhibitors as in non-small cell lung cancer (Sutherland et al, 2022) and other alternative immunotherapeutic approaches may be of interest.
L118: A meta-analysis of 27 studies, enrolling a total of 2792 patients, suggests heterogeneity in the prevalence of PD-L1 expression across studies. No statistical association has been confirmed, although positive PD-L1 expression seems to be a favorable prognostic factor for SCLC (Acheampong et al, 2020). The sensitivity of immunotherapy may be associated with the disease itself, according to the molecular classification biology of SCLC (Caliman et al, 2023). Further studies are necessary to determine which patient groups would benefit the most from immunotherapy treatment.
L119: Currently there are no approved therapies for patients failing 2 or more prior lines of therapy (NCCN SCLC Guidelines, 2023; Giffin et al, 2021). Patients receiving best supportive care in third line had an extremely poor survival outcome with a median OS of 0.9 months, whereas patients receiving systemic therapy in third line had a median OS of 4.4 months (Coutinho et al, 2019).

L128: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L129: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's presentation of current treatment options. Cytotoxic chemotherapies are expected to have differential tumor response based on a patient's "sensitivity" to platinum-based chemotherapy and given this, efficacy is usually assessed separately in subgroups of platinum-sensitive or platinum-resistant with the former group being generally defined as patients who have had disease progression \ge 90 days after completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy and the latter group being defined as patients who have progression &lt; 90 days after completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy. Based on data from single arm studies with topotecan using the more standard cut-off of \ge 90 days for platinum-sensitive vs. platinum-resistant, reported response rates ranged from 11-31% for patients with platinum-sensitive disease and 2-7% in patients with platinum-resistant disease. For lurbinectedin, accelerated approval was granted based on an ORR of 35%; ORR was 43% (95% CI: 31%, 57%) in the platinum-sensitive subgroup and 13% (95% CI: 5%, 27%) in the platinum-resistant subgroup.

L147: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L148: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L149: An OSI audit was conducted for this BLA. The review division (DO2) and OSI selected three investigator sites, and the imaging contract research organization (CRO) (b)(4) for clinical inspection. The three investigators selected were Dr. Byoung Chul Cho (Site ID #25054), Dr. Myung-Ju Ahn (Site ID #66264) and Dr. Enrique Felip Font (Site ID #29404).
L150: The inspection of Dr. Ahn revealed protocol violations, including enrollment of two patients who did not meet eligibility criteria and underreporting of adverse events (AEs). Specifically, Patient (b)(6) had a new diagnosis of adenocarcinoma and should not have been enrolled in the study, as the patient did not fulfill inclusion criterion to have “histologically or cytologically confirmed relapse/refractory SCLC.” This patient received the tarlatamab 100 mg dose. Patient (b)(6) had abdominal aneurysm with thrombosis and should have been excluded from study based on the exclusion criteria. Based on the available information, this protocol deviation did not appear to have impact on this patient’s safety.
L151: Inspection of Dr. Ahn’s site also revealed underreporting of AEs in 14 out of 19 patients enrolled at the site. A total of 61 AEs including three Grade 3 AEs and two Grade 5 AEs (death due to underlying disease) were not reported to the BLA in the Applicant’s data listings. The root cause of the violations was due to lack of human resources at the site as well as failure in the Applicant’s monitoring activities. On January 31, 2024, OSI, in conjunction with the review Division, requested that the Applicant provide a root-cause and impact analysis for the eligibility and AE discrepancies at Dr. Ahn’s site, and to perform a source record review of subject’s eligibility and adverse events for all subjects enrolled at all 5 sites in South Korea. Based on the Applicant’s source record review of the Korean sites, an additional 42 unreported AEs at 3 of the 4 remaining sites and involving 12 patients were identified.
L152: Root cause analysis showed the following factors that caused inadequate AE reporting by the site: 1) lack of Good Documentation Practices for adverse events tracking – study coordinators entered adverse events information on an undisclosed source document, 2) Lack of electronic medical record (EMR) access by study coordinators - study coordinators could not enter adverse events into EMR, 3) Undisclosed source documents to the clinical research associate (CRA) - source document was not disclosed to the CRA when inquiring about potential adverse events under-reporting and 4) Under-resourcing of study coordinators at site during high enrollment periods. Site 66264 remains on hold in the Applicant’s clinical trials until the Applicant is satisfied that the robustness of the site’s processes will prevent recurrence of GCP inspection findings identified.
L153: The following preventative measures have been implemented by the Applicant regarding AE reporting:
L154: - Review the root cause of each missing AE at each of the impacted sites to identify and correct the unique causes at each site.
- Document the availability of the site AE reporting procedure and ensure site's training logs are completed and up to date with respect to the current versions of the procedure.
- Plan to update the Monitoring Plan to incorporate regular verification of the site AE reporting procedures and associated site staff training by April 30, 2024.
- Re-training of AE reporting for all sites on study.
- Implementation of additional edit checks in Electronic Data Capture (EDC) for AE reporting verification
- Plan to add the study specific risk of AE reporting and associated mitigations and actions into the risk management system by April 30, 2024.
L155: Inspections of Drs. Cho and Font's sites also revealed unreported AEs that are unlikely to significantly affect overall reliability of safety or efficacy data for these sites. The large number of underreported AEs by the Korean sites, not captured during the Applicant's monitoring activities, is a major concern and put into question the integrity of the safety data submitted to support the approval of tarlatamab for the proposed indication.
L156: The Applicant has acknowledged the deficiencies and decided to further expand their review to conduct 100% source data verification (SDV)/source data review (SDR) for all sites involved in 20200491. Based on SDR of imaging records, 25 previously unsubmitted scans were identified in 17 patients. Of the 25 scans, 23 were unscheduled scans and were previously not submitted as these scans were completed as part of standard of care or for patient AE assessment. Of the remaining 2 scans, one was a scheduled brain MRI that was not submitted for central review due to a technical imaging upload error and the other was a baseline abdominal MRI that was not submitted as the site erroneously indicated that only baseline CT was available.
L157: These unsubmitted scans were submitted to BICR for review and from this the Applicant updated the ORR by BICR from 40% to 41% for the tarlatamab 10 mg group and from 32% to 33% in the tarlatamab 100 mg group. The median DoR did not change in the 10 mg group and changed from 8.1 to 7.6 months in the 100 mg group. However, upon further review by FDA, none of the newly identified scans that were submitted factored into the update of response for these patients and the update of ORR was from re-adjudication of previous target lesion assessments. Given this, FDA did not agree to update the ORR the DoR summary statistics would be based on the previous ORR by BICR of 40%.

L182: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L183: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L184: Tarlatamab (AMG 757) is a bispecific T cell engager that binds to delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) and CD3. DLL3 is a Notch ligand that is expressed during fetal development but has limited expression on the cell surface in healthy adult tissues. DLL3 has also been reported to be expressed on the cell surface of some cancers, including small cell lung cancer (SCLC). CD3 is expressed on T cells, and upon simultaneously binding to the surface of DLL3-expressing target cells and CD3 on T cells, tarlatamab promotes T cell activation and T cell-dependent lysis of DLL3-expressing cells, including SCLC cells. The Applicant proposes to use tarlatamab at 1 mg on Day 1, then 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter. The proposed indication is for the treatment of adult patients with SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
L185: As assessed by surface plasmon resonance (SPR), tarlatamab bound human and monkey CD3 with K_Ds of 14.9 nM and 12 nM, respectively. Tarlatamab bound CHO cells expressing human or monkey DLL3 with K_Ds of 0.64 nM and 0.5 nM, respectively. Tarlatamab also bound human and monkey T cells at a concentration of 50 ng/mL but did not bind T cells from mice, rats, rabbits, dogs, or minipigs. The cynomolgus monkey was selected for toxicology testing given that tarlatamab similarly bound human and monkey CD3 and DLL3 but did not bind CD3 from other species. However, the monkey may not be pharmacologically relevant considering DLL3 is expressed intracellularly in normal cells and tissues. It is unknown if other conditions or disease states can lead to expression of DLL3 on the surface of some cells.
L186: The ability of tarlatamab to activate T cells and mediate T cell cytotoxicity was evaluated in in vitro assays. In co-cultures with human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) and DLL3-expressing human small cell lung cancer cell lines, tarlatamab resulted in upregulation of T cell activation markers and cytokine release, as well as cytotoxicity of DLL3-positive target cells. Tarlatamab mediated T cell lysis of several human SCLC cell lines with EC_{50}s of 32 pM to 161 pM. Additionally, the ability of tarlatamab to mediate cytotoxicity of DLL3-positive target cells positively correlated with levels of DLL3 expressed on the surface of target cells. Tarlatamab also demonstrated ability to activate CD4 and CD8 T cells in co-cultures with DLL3-expressing human cancer cell lines as assessed by increased expression of T cell activation markers, such as CD25 and CD69, and cytotoxicity markers, perforin and granzyme B, as well as release of cytokines, including the proinflammatory cytokines IL-2, IL-6, TNF-\alpha, and IFN\gamma. Tarlatamab did not mediate cytokine release from human PBMCs when co-cultured with target cells lacking expression of DLL3.
L187: In the presence of dexamethasone, a glucocorticoid used clinically to antagonize severe inflammatory reactions, the ability of tarlatamab to mediate T cell activation and cytotoxicity of DLL3-expressing human SCLC cell lines was reduced approximately 3-fold. Tarlatamab-mediated release of IFN\gamma, TNF-\alpha, IL-10, and IL-6 were also reduced in co-cultures of human PBMCs and SCLC cell lines compared to cultures that were not treated with dexamethasone.
L188: Consistent with its mutated Fc region, tarlatamab demonstrated low affinity binding to Fc\gammaR1 and no binding to Fc\gammaRII, Fc\gammaRIII, or C1q, thus tarlatamab is not expected to mediate Fc-effector functions; the mutations in the Fc region did not affect the ability of tarlatamab to bind the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn). In an in vitro assay, tarlatamab resulted in low level upregulation of activation markers CD25 and CD69 on human T cells when PBMCs were cultured alone or in the presence of HCT-116 cells lacking expression of DLL3, suggesting the potential for tarlatamab to mediate T cell activation independently of DLL3 engagement via binding of the ScFc region of tarlatamab to Fc\gammaR1 on target cells and simultaneous binding to CD3 on T cells; however, upregulation of CD25 and CD69 were abolished in the presence of physiological levels of human IgG. This finding suggests that competitive binding from human IgG can block tarlatamab binding to Fc\gammaRs, thereby inhibiting target-independent T cell activation. Notably, antigen-independent upregulation of T cell activation markers did not correlate with cytokine release from unstimulated PBMCs in an in vitro assay described above, further indicating that potential interactions with Fc\gammaR1 are weak and are unlikely to lead to T cell activation or Fc-mediated effector functions.
L189: Tarlatamab inhibited tumor growth in multiple mouse tumor models. Because tarlatamab does not bind mouse CD3, the in vivo anti-tumor activity of tarlatamab was evaluated using immunodeficient mice administered human PBMCs or T cells by IV infusion prior to treatment with tarlatamab. In orthotopic models of SCLC, tarlatamab inhibited tumor growth when administered at doses \ge0.1 mg/kg once weekly via intraperitoneal injection. Anti-tumor activity in SCLC models was associated with expanded CD4 and CD8 T cell populations in tumors from mice administered tarlatamab compared to tumors from mice treated with an isotype control bispecific antibody. Overall, based on primary pharmacology data the established pharmacologic class (EPC) of tarlatamab is bispecific delta-like ligand (DLL3)-directed CD3 T-cell engager.
L190: The Applicant evaluated the safety of tarlatamab administered intravenously, the intended route of administration, using cynomolgus monkeys in GLP-compliant 1- and 3-month repeat-dose toxicology studies, as well as a non-GLP 10-day study. No mortalities occurred in these studies at doses up to 4500 \mug/kg, the maximal feasible dose that could be administered to monkeys. Findings in these studies included decreases in lymphocytes at doses \ge1000 \mug/kg in all repeat-dose toxicology studies in monkeys; this finding improved after a drug-free period. Changes in lymphocytes also correlated with decreases in total CD3, CD4, and CD8 T cell
L191: populations in monkeys administered \ge 100 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} or 4500 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} in the 10-day and 1-month studies, respectively; these changes also demonstrated reversibility. In a 1-month toxicology study in mice administered muS757, a tarlatamab surrogate that binds mouse DLL3 and CD3, decreased lymphocytes were also noted at \ge 500 \text{ mg}/\text{kg} muS757 and correlated with decreased lymphoid cellularity in the lymph nodes. Additionally, decreased neutrophils were noted in monkeys at 4500 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} in the 1-month study and in mice administered \ge 500 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} muS757. Other hematological changes, including decreased red blood cells, hemoglobin, and hematocrit were noted at doses \ge 100 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} in the 10-day study in monkeys. The changes in red blood cell parameters were not noted in the GLP studies of up to 3-months duration; however, anemia was observed in humans. Consistent with nonclinical findings, decreases in lymphocytes and neutropenia have occurred clinically and the IMDELLTRA (tarlatamab) label includes a warning for neutropenia.
L192: Signs of an inflammatory response were noted in the 10-day study, including increases in C-reactive protein (CRP), MCP-1, and IFN\gamma at doses \ge 100 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg}. Transient increases in heart rate occurred in the 1-month monkey study at \ge 500 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg} following the first dose, but this finding was not noted at any time point in the 3-month study in monkeys. At doses \ge 50 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg}, minimal to mild mononuclear cell infiltrates were noted in the pituitary gland of monkeys; however, a tissue-cross reactivity study using tissues from humans and monkeys demonstrated only cytoplasmic DLL3 expression in pituitary and brain tissues, indicating DLL3 in these tissues is not expected to be accessible to tarlatamab. Additionally, minimal to mild mononuclear and/or mixed cell infiltrates were observed in multiple tissues in all toxicology studies in monkeys.
L193: The Applicant did not conduct genetic toxicology studies as they generally are not appropriate for antibody-based therapies. In addition, the Applicant did not conduct dedicated studies to assess fertility. In general, these studies are not needed to support a marketing application for a drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. There were no adverse or notable effects on male or female reproductive organs in the repeat-dose toxicology studies conducted in sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys.
L194: Based on mechanism of action of tarlatamab, the expression of DLL3 during fetal development, and data from a literature-based risk assessment, the pharmacology/toxicology team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for IMDELLTRA. Tarlatamab causes T cell-dependent depletion of DLL3-expressing cells, and T cell activation may result in cytokine release and associated inflammatory effects that may adversely affect the ability to maintain a pregnancy. Two administrations of a tarlatamab surrogate, muS757, that binds mouse CD3 and DLL3, to pregnant mice on gestational days (GD) 6 and GD 13 did not result in maternal toxicity or embryo-fetal development toxicity at doses \le 4500 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg}. muS757 was detected in fetal serum at concentrations up to 30-times higher than maternal serum concentrations, indicating the potential for tarlatamab to cross the placenta. Additionally, studies using genetically engineered mice demonstrate that DLL3 loss-of-function mutations or deletion of DLL3
L195: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L196: disrupted somitogenesis during embryogenesis leading to disorganization of the axial skeleton and disruption of vertebral formation, suggesting that fetal exposure to tarlatamab may increase the risk of abnormal skeletal development (Chapman et al., 2011, Dunwoodie et al., 2002, Kusumi et al. 1998). In these studies, DLL3 was only detected intracellularly during embryogenesis, which may explain the lack of findings in the embryo-fetal development study in mice using muS757 given that tarlatamab is expected to target DLL3 expressed on the cell surface.
L197: Human immunoglobulin G (IgG) is known to cross the placenta via FcRn; tarlatamab can bind FcRn, therefore, tarlatamab has the potential to be transmitted from the mother to the developing fetus. Consistent with the recommendation for non-genotoxic drugs that are teratogenic or embryo-fetal lethal as described in the FDA Guidance for Industry Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations, and considering the half-life of 11.2 days for tarlatamab, FDA recommends advising females of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with IMDELLTRA and for 2 months after the last dose. The Applicant did not evaluate the presence of tarlatamab in milk; however, maternal IgG is known to be present in human milk. The effects of local gastrointestinal exposure and limited systemic exposure in the breastfed child to IMDELLTRA are unknown. Because of the potential serious adverse effects of IMDELLTRA on a breastfed child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed during treatment with IMDELLTRA and for 2 months after the last dose.
L198: ## **Recommendation:**
L199: There are no outstanding issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective that would prevent the approval of IMDELLTRA.

L205: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L206: ## **Primary pharmacology**
L207: Tarlatamab (also designated as AMG 757) is a bispecific T-cell engager (BiTE®) molecule that
L208: 50
L209: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
L210: *Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.*
L211: targets the tumor-associated antigen delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3). Tarlatamab consists of 2 single-chain variable fragment binding domains, specific for DLL3 and the T-cell receptor (TCR)-associated protein cluster of differentiation 3 (CD3), fused to a silenced fragment crystallizable (Fc) immunoglobulin domain to extend serum half-life. Tarlatamab is designed to simultaneously bind to DLL3 that is expressed on the surface of cancer cells and CD3 expressed on the surface of T cells.
L212: The mechanism of action of tarlatamab is T cell-dependent lysis of tumor cells. The pharmacological effect of tarlatamab is mediated by specific redirection of T cells to kill DLL3-expressing tumor cells. The primary pharmacology of tarlatamab was assessed in SCLC cell lines in vitro and in SCLC xenograft models in vivo.
L213: Tarlatamab binding to human and cynomolgus monkey DLL3 was assessed by flow cytometry, and binding to human and cynomolgus monkey CD3 was measured by surface plasmon resonance (SPR) analysis (Table 3; Study Number: 122717/ electronic common technical document [eCTD] Location: Module 4.2.1.1).
L214: The binding of tarlatamab to Fc-gamma receptors (Fc\gammaR), the neonatal Fc receptor complex (FcRn/B2M) and complement component 1q (C1q) was assessed by SPR analysis (Table 3; Study Number: 157053/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1).
L215: While the single-chain Fc region of tarlatamab showed low affinity binding to Fc\gammaR1/CD64, it did not interact with Fc\gammaR1a/CD32a and Fc\gammaR11a/CD16a. The interaction with Fc\gammaR1/CD64 was characterized by very fast association and dissociation rates, and the equilibrium dissociation constant (K_D) value was about 7000-fold lower than that of a control monoclonal antibody, rituximab (Study 157053). Tarlatamab did not bind to human C1q (Study 157053). Thus, tarlatamab is not expected to mediate effector functions such as antibody-dependent cell mediated cytotoxicity and complement-dependent cytotoxicity. Furthermore, tarlatamab showed a pH-dependent interaction with human FcRn/B2M heterodimer receptors with a K_D value of 444 nM, which was comparable to rituximab (345 nM) (Study 157053).

L221: Tarlatamab engaged T cells from human or cynomolgus monkey to effectively lyse SCLC cells in vitro (Table 4; Study Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1). The cross-reactivity of tarlatamab to cynomolgus monkey shown by similar binding affinity and cytotoxic activity supported selection of cynomolgus monkey for nonclinical toxicology studies.

L225: Bispecific binding of tarlatamab to DLL3 on tumor cells and the TCR/CD3 complex on T cells induces similar effects as antigen-specific recognition of peptide-major histocompatibility complex by the TCR of T cells. Thus, tarlatamab initiates activity of cytotoxic T lymphocytes through the upregulation of key molecules of T-cell activation and transient cytokine production by T cells (Study 122717).
L226: In co-cultures of DLL3-expressing SHP-77 and human T cells, tarlatamab-induced dose-dependent upregulation of T-cell activation markers, including CD69 (Figure 1; Study Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1).

Each data point represents the mean of duplicate measurements. Error bars represent the standard deviation.

L231: Activated cytotoxic T cells secrete inflammatory cytokines following TCR engagement. The ability of tarlatamab to promote cytokine release was investigated in co-cultures of human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMC) with DLL3-expressing target cells (Study Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1). Human T cell-induced target cell killing was accompanied by the dose-dependent release of interferon gamma (IFN-\gamma), interleukin (IL)-6, IL-10, tumor necrosis factor (TNF) as well as IL-2 in the presence of DLL3-positive SHP-77 cells (Figure 2B). Cytokine release was highly specific for the co-cultures that contained DLL3-expressing cells, as no cytokines were detected in co-cultures of human effector cells with DLL3-negative HCT-116 cells after exposure to tarlatamab (Figure 2A; Study 122717).

L236: Tarlatamab bound to cynomolgus monkey DLL3 and CD3 with similar binding affinity as to human DLL3 and CD3, and induced T-cell activation, transient cytokine production and T cell-mediated lysis of target cells in co-cultures of cynomolgus monkey effector cells and target cells that expressed cynomolgus monkey DLL3 comparable to that in the human system (Study Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1). Furthermore, the cynomolgus monkey was considered a relevant animal model based on similarity of DLL3 expression in cynomolgus monkey tissues as compared with human.
L237: Following the proposal of the FDA, a murine surrogate molecule of tarlatamab, designated muS757, was developed to evaluate potential effects of a DLL3-targeting BiTE molecule on embryofetal development in mice. The comparability assessment of muS757 demonstrated similar binding affinity, bioactivity (Study Numbers 122717 (tarlatamab) and 154479 (muS757) / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1), pharmacokinetic properties (Study Number 154480 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.2.2), and tolerability profile to tarlatamab (Study Number 153960 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.2.3), indicating that it is a suitable surrogate molecule for embryofetal toxicology assessment. The FDA,
L238: (b) (4) agreed that, based on these data, muS757 is a valid surrogate for tarlatamab for embryofetal development toxicity assessment.
L239: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L240: In vivo, tarlatamab activity was evaluated in xenograft models of SCLC. The SHP-77 and NCI-H82 cell lines that had been characterized in the in vitro studies were used in orthotopic xenograft models. The SHP-77 cells primarily formed tumors in the lung to mimic primary SCLC tumors, and the NCI-H82 cells formed tumor metastases predominantly in liver, characteristic of typical SCLC metastasis. In addition, 2 patient-derived xenograft (PDX) tumor models that were used, LXFS 1129 and LXFS 538, were representative of the DLL3 expression levels and tumor heterogeneity observed in human SCLC tumors (Giffin et al, 2021).
L241: The tarlatamab mechanism of action requires the simultaneous binding to DLL3 on tumor cells and CD3 on T cells. As tarlatamab does not cross-react to mouse CD3, the xenograft models used in the nonclinical pharmacology studies were conducted in immunocompromised mice (non-obese diabetic/severe combined immunodeficiency, interleukin 2 receptor gamma [NSG]) that were injected with activated, expanded human T cells before treatment with tarlatamab. Together, the xenograft models enabled direct testing of the clinical molecule tarlatamab and facilitated evaluation of pharmacodynamic activity in tumors (T-cell infiltration and/or expansion, T-cell activation, and tumor cell apoptosis) and antitumor activity.

following this page
L243: 55
L244: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L245: *Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.*
L246: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L247: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. In addition to these findings, the FDA notes the following results:
L248: At a concentration of 50 ng/mL, tarlatamab bound human and cynomolgus T cells, but did not bind T cells from mice, rats, rabbits, dogs, or minipigs (Study# R20140017). Binding to human and monkey CD3 and DLL3 was further characterized in in vitro assays. Tarlatamab bound CHO cells expressing human or monkey DLL3 with K_D values of 0.64 nM and 0.5 nM, respectively; tarlatamab did not bind untransfected CHO cells (Study# 122717). As assessed by SPR, tarlatamab bound human CD3 with a K_D of 14.9 nM and monkey CD3 with a K_D of 12 nM. Additionally, tarlatamab demonstrated similar binding to human and monkey FcRn in a qualitative SPR assay (Study# 122717). Given that tarlatamab demonstrated the ability to bind monkey CD3 and DLL3 similarly to human CD3 and DLL3, the monkey was selected for toxicity testing of tarlatamab.
L249: Tarlatamab mediated lysis of multiple human small cell lung cancer cell lines endogenously expressing DLL3 on the cell surface by human and monkey PBMCs but did not mediate lysis of HCT-116 human colon cancer cells that do not express DLL3 (Study # 122717). Cytotoxicity was also dependent on the expression level of DLL3 on target cells as demonstrated by findings of lower EC_{50} values in assays with target cells expressing high levels of DLL3 (SHP-77, EC_{50}=32 pM) compared to assays using target cells with intermediate (COR-L279, EC_{50} = 57 pm) or low levels of DLL3 (H-2171, EC_{50}=161 pM).
L250: Tarlatamab-mediated T cell lysis of DLL3+ target cells was time-dependent with cytotoxicity initially observed after 16 hours of culture and maximum cytotoxicity observed after 48 hours of culture. Though maximum cytotoxicity increased over time, EC_{50} values remained consistent across the incubation period with EC_{50} values of 23.2 pM to 41.4 pM for co-cultures with DMS 79 cells and EC_{50} values of 5.5 to 7.7 pM for co-cultures with SHP-77 cells (Study # 122717).
L251: The effect of donor variability on in vitro T cell mediated lysis was assessed by co-culturing human PBMCs (n=12) with DLL3+ SHP-77 target cells at an effector-to target cell ratio (E:T) of 5:1 for 48 hours, then evaluating target cell viability by flow cytometry. PBMCs from all donors mediated cytotoxicity of SHP-77 cells with an average EC_{50} of 26.4 pM and maximum cytotoxicity of 74% to 86% (Study # 122717).
L252: The ability of tarlatamab to mediate lysis of DLL3+ target cell by CD4 or CD8 T cells was evaluated in an in vitro assay (Study# R20140017). CD4 and CD8 T cells were enriched from human PBMCs and co-cultured with SHP-77 cells at an E:T of 5:1 for up to 72 hours, then cytotoxicity was assessed by flow cytometry. Though maximal cytotoxicity was similar after 24 to 72 hours of co-culture in assays using CD4 or CD8 T cells, EC_{50} values for CD4-mediated lysis
L253: (EC_{50}=29.7 pM to 47.8 pM) were slightly higher than EC_{50} values for CD8-mediated lysis (EC_{50}=3.8 to 16.1 pM). These results indicate that both CD4 and CD8 T cells contribute to tarlatamab-mediated lysis of DLL3+ target cells by T cells. Similar findings were also observed in cultures using CD4 and CD8 T cells from monkey PBMCs (Study # 122717).
L254: In the presence of DLL3-expressing SHP-77 cells, treatment with tarlatamab resulted in increased expression of CD71, PD-1, and PD-L1, in addition to CD69 expression on CD4 and CD8 T cells (Study # 122717). These findings further indicate the ability of tarlatamab to activate CD4 and CD8 T cells.
L255: In a separate assay, treatment with tarlatamab resulted in upregulation of activation markers CD25 and CD69 on human T cells when PBMCs were cultured alone or in the presence of HCT-116 cells lacking expression of DLL3, suggesting tarlatamab may mediate T cell activation independently of DLL3 engagement; however, upregulation of CD25 and CD69 were abolished when physiological levels of human IgG were added to the assay conditions (Study # 122717). These findings suggest that low affinity binding of the ScFc region of tarlatamab to Fc\gammaR1 on target cells and simultaneous binding to CD3 on T cells can induce T cell activation but competitive binding from human IgG can block tarlatamab binding to Fc\gammaRs, thereby inhibiting target-independent T cell activation. These findings were further confirmed in an assay using an anti-CD64 blocking antibody (Study # 122717).
L256: Dose dependent cytokine release was also observed in co-cultures with H-2171, NCI-H889, and SHP-77 human SCLC cell lines (Study # 122717). Higher maximal cytokine release was noted in co-cultures with SHP-77 cells endogenously expressing high levels of DLL3 compared to H-2171 cells expressing comparatively lower levels of DLL3, further suggesting that tarlatamab activity is dependent upon expression levels of DLL3 on target cells (Study # 122717). Cytokine release was also time-dependent with maximal release of IL-2, IL-4, IL-10, TNF-\alpha, and IFN\gamma occurring after approximately 16 hours of culture, then decreasing by 24 hours; however, IFN\gamma levels remained elevated through 72 hours of culture. Comparatively, IL-6 release peaked after 48 hours of cultures.
L257: In addition to increased expression of activation markers in co-cultures with DLL3+ target cells, tarlatamab resulted in increased expression of granzyme B and perforin, markers of cytotoxic activity on T cells (Study # 122717). Human PBMCs were co-cultured with either SHP-77 or DMS 79 human SCLC cells at an E:T of 2:1 in the presence of tarlatamab for up to 72 hours, then expression of perforin and granzyme B were assessed by flow cytometry. Up to 50% of CD8 T cells were positive for perforin in the presence of tarlatamab, though only a minor percentage of CD4 T cells expressed perforin after co-culture with DLL3-expressing target cells in the presence of tarlatamab. Tarlatamab also resulted in increased expression of granzyme B in CD4 and CD8 T cells in a concentration-dependent manner with similar maximal increases in both T cell populations (Study # 122717).
L258: Dexamethasone may be used clinically to antagonize severe inflammatory responses. To evaluate the effect of dexamethasone on the activity of tarlatamab, human PBMCs were co-cultured with DMS 79 or SHP-77 human small cell lung cancer cell lines in the presence of tarlatamab and dexamethasone, then T cell activation and target cell cytotoxicity were assessed by flow cytometry and supernatants were collected to evaluate cytokine release. Results were compared to co-cultures treated with tarlatamab but not dexamethasone. Though tarlatamab resulted in concentration-dependent increased expression of the T cell activation markers CD69 and CD25 in cultures treated with and without dexamethasone, maximal responses were lower and EC50 values were approximately 3.3- and 3.7-times higher for co-cultures with DMS 79 and SHP-77 cells, respectively. Treatment with dexamethasone also resulted in lower release of IFN\gamma, TNF-\alpha, IL-10, and IL-6. These findings suggest dexamethasone may reduce tarlatamab-mediated T cell activation in the presence of DLL3+ target cells (Study# 122718).
L259: As described by the Applicant above, tarlatamab inhibited growth of multiple DLL3-expressing tumors in mice when administered intraperitoneally at doses \ge0.1 mg/kg. Anti-tumor activity of tarlatamab was associated with expansion of tumor-infiltrating CD4 and CD8 T cells in mice bearing SHP-77 or NCI-H82 SCLC tumors compared to mice treated with an isotype control (Study#s R201330093 and R20190094).
L260: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L261: ## The Applicant's Position:
L262: The DLL3 expression profile was analyzed in normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues:
L263: - DLL3 transcript variants exist that do not contain the sequences recognized by tarlatamab (Study Numbers: 124207; 124208/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
- The quantification of canonical DLL3 transcripts that contain the tarlatamab binding region indicates extremely low levels of expression in a small subset of normal tissues (Study Numbers: 122642; 123658; 124208; / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
- The DLL3 protein expression is extremely low in this limited subset of tissues (Study Numbers: 123377 and 123293/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
- The expression pattern appears to be restricted to the cytoplasm although membranous expression below the limit of detection might be possible (Study Number: 123377/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
L264: Therefore, it is anticipated that tarlatamab administration is not associated with secondary pharmacodynamics leading to normal tissue damage since DLL3 in the cytoplasm would not be accessible to tarlatamab.
L265: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L266: To evaluate the potential for off-target activation of platelets, tarlatamab was tested in a flow cytometry assay for platelet activation at concentrations of 125, 41.7, 13.9, 4.6, 1.5, and 0.5 µg/mL in both human and cynomolgus monkey whole blood. No activation of platelets was observed in either human or cynomolgus monkey whole blood (Study Number 122111 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
L267: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L268: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. Additional findings include:
L269: Non-canonical DLL3 mRNA transcript variants were identified in normal human and monkey tissues, but these transcripts do not contain sequences coding for tarlatamab binding regions of DLL3 (Study# 123658, 124207). DLL3 mRNA transcripts for canonical, full length DLL3 were identified at low levels in the optic nerve of monkeys and the brains of humans and monkeys (Study#s 124208, 123658, 122642).
L270: As assessed by immunohistochemistry, cytoplasmic DLL3 staining was noted in the pituitary, pancreatic islets, and in neurons in several regions of the brain (cerebrum, cerebellum, hypothalamus, hippocampus), though staining was noted as generally weak to occasionally mild in intensity (Study# 123377). These findings suggest that though DLL3 may be expressed in some tissues, DLL3 is retained intracellularly and would not be expected to be accessible for tarlatamab.
L271: ## Safety Pharmacology
L272: Consistent with the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use Guideline: Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceuticals S9 (ICH, 2010) no stand-alone safety pharmacology studies were conducted for tarlatamab. Electrocardiographic assessments, respiratory rate, body temperature measurements, and neurologic examinations were included on the toxicology studies in the cynomolgus monkey (Section 5.5.1 / Study Numbers 121423; 121424; 122516 / eCTD location: Module 4.2.3.2).
L273: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L274: The FDA agrees.
L275: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L276: ## The Applicant's Position:
L277: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L278: The pharmacokinetics (PK) of tarlatamab was characterized after single intravenous (IV) bolus injections (Study 121985 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.2.2), short IV infusions (Study 121424 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.3.2), IVs slow bolus administration (Study 122516 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.3.2), and subcutaneous administration in cynomolgus monkeys (Study 157064 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.3.6). After a single IV bolus injection, serum concentrations of tarlatamab declined in a biphasic manner with a mean terminal half-life of 234 hours (Study 121985 / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.2.2).
L279: The toxicokinetics (TK) of tarlatamab was characterized in cynomolgus monkeys after weekly IV doses of 50, 500, and 4500 mg/kg for up to 3 months. The studies have been described in detail in Section 2.2 of Module 2.6.6, Toxicology Written Summary. Briefly, tarlatamab exposure increased with dose and with repeated dosing. No difference (within 2-fold) in exposure, as assessed by the area under the curve from the time of dosing to the last measurable concentration (AUC_{last}) and calculated drug concentration at time 0 after IV bolus dose (C_0) was observed between male and female animals.
L280: The TK of muS757 was characterized in BALB/c mice after weekly IV doses of 50, 500, and 4500 mg/kg for up to 28 days. The study has been described in detail in Section 2.2 of Module 2.6.6, Toxicology Written Summary. Briefly, muS757 exposure was dose-proportional and increased over time, particularly at the \ge 500 mg/kg dose levels. There was no sex-based difference in exposure in AUC_{last} and maximum observed concentration (C_{max}) on days 1 and 22.
L281: muS757 exposure increased approximately in proportion to dose in both females and fetuses. At the single sampling time point included (GD 13, 120 hr) fetal serum concentrations were 22.6- to 29.5-fold higher than female serum concentrations (Reference Table 8 and Table 9).
