L1490: ## **Dose Interruption/Reduction Due to Adverse Effects**
L1491: ## Data:
L1492: Adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab were reported for 157 subjects (34.4) treated with tarlatamab monotherapy, with the following preferred terms reported for ≥ 2% of subjects: CRS (6.1%), COVID-19 (3.9%), neutropenia (2.8%), fatigue (2.6%), and pyrexia (2.0%) (ISS Table 14-6.2.17).
L1493: The incidence of adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab was lower in the monotherapy 10 mg group (49 subjects [30.6%]) than in the 100 mg group (71 subjects [43.6%]). The most frequently reported (≥ 5% in either treatment group) adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab occurred at a lower incidence in the 10 mg group compared with the 100 mg group: CRS (3.8% vs 9.2%), COVID-19 (3.8% vs 6.1%), and neutropenia (0.6% vs 5.5%).
L1494: ## Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
L1495: Overall, adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab were reported for [REDACTED](b) (4) treated with tarlatamab monotherapy, including 57 subjects (30.5%) with SCLC treated with 10 mg tarlatamab and 71 subjects (43.6%) with SCLC treated with 100 mg tarlatamab. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, overall, the most frequently reported (≥ 2% of subjects) adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab were CRS (5.8%), COVID-19 (3.9%), neutropenia (2.9%), and fatigue (2.7%).
L1496: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1497: The incidence of adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab was lower in the monotherapy 10 mg group (30.6%) than in the 100 mg group (43.6%). The most frequently reported adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab

## {Tarlatamab}

L1500: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1501: In FDA's analysis of the pooled safety population, adverse events leading to dose interruptions occurred in 55 patients (27%). The most frequently reported adverse events leading to dose interruptions in \ge 2\% of patients included fatigue (3.2%), cytokine release syndrome (2.7%) and respiratory tract infection (2.1%). All events were coded as dose reduced but based on the narratives, all were dose interruptions (dose reductions were not permitted).
L1502: ## **Significant Adverse Events**
L1503: ## **Data:**
L1504: In the monotherapy studies, grade \ge 3 adverse events were reported with similar frequency overall (b) (4) and in the 10 and 100 mg dose groups (63.1% and 64.4%, respectively) (Table 53). Overall, the most frequently reported (&gt; 5\% of subjects) grade \ge 3 adverse events were anemia (7.4%), fatigue (6.3%), hyponatremia (6.1%), and decreased lymphocyte count (5.3%) (Table 13 of Module 2.7.4, SCS and ISS Table 14-6.2.9). Some of the grade \ge 3 adverse events were reported at a lower frequency in the 10 mg group than in the 100 mg group, including neutropenia (3.1% vs 6.7%), CRS (1.3% vs 6.1%), and white blood cell count decreased (0.6% vs 4.3%). Lymphopenia occurred at a higher frequency in the 10 mg group (7.5%) than in the 100 mg group (3.1%). The types of grade \ge 3 adverse events reported with combination therapy were generally similar to those reported for the monotherapy population (Section 2.1.2.2 of Module 2.7.4, Summary of Clinical Safety).
L1505: ## ***Safety Update as of 02 October 2023***
L1506: The types of grade \ge 3 adverse events for the Monotherapy Safety Analysis Set were generally consistent between the initial ISS analysis and 90-day Safety Update. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, overall, the most frequently reported (\ge 5\% of subjects) grade \ge 3 adverse events by preferred term for subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy included anemia (7.2%), fatigue (6.4%), hyponatremia (6.6%), and decreased lymphocyte count (5.6%) (90DSU ISS Table 14-6.2.9). In contrast to the initial ISS analysis, lymphopenia (5.2%) was also reported for \ge 5\% of subjects.
L1507: For the 10 mg target dose, consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported
L1508: NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
L1509: {Tarlatamab}
L1510: (\ge 5\% of subjects) grade \ge 3 adverse events by preferred term for subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were lymphopenia (8.0%); decreased lymphocyte count, anemia, and hyponatremia (6.4% each); and fatigue (5.9%).
L1511: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L1512: For the 100 mg target dose, consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported (\ge 5\% of subjects) grade \ge 3 adverse events by preferred term for subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were neutropenia, anemia, fatigue, and hyponatremia (6.7% each); and CRS (6.1%).
L1513: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1514: The types of grade \ge 3 adverse events reported for subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab at the 10 mg dose were generally similar to those reported for subjects treated at the 100 mg dose, although the incidences of certain grade \ge 3 events (hyponatremia, neutropenia, and CRS) were higher in the 100 mg group than in the 10 mg group. The incidence of grade \ge 3 adverse events with the 90-day safety update was similar to that seen in the initial ISS analysis.
L1515: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1516: FDA's analysis of significant adverse events in the pooled population included CRS, neurologic toxicity including ICANS, and cytopenias, which are further described in section 8.2.5 of this review. Other significant adverse events included infections, hepatotoxicity and hypersensitivity which are included in the Warnings and Precaution section of the label.
L1517: Infections in the pooled safety population, including opportunistic infections, occurred in 41% of patients who received tarlatamab. Grade 3 or 4 infections occurred in 13% of patients. The most frequent infections were COVID-19 (9%, majority during the COVID-19 pandemic), urinary tract infection (10%), pneumonia (9%), respiratory tract infection (3.2%), and candida infection (3.2%).
L1518: Hepatotoxicity in the pooled safety population included elevated ALT occurring in 42% of patients with Grade 3 or 4 ALT elevation occurring in 2.1% of tarlatamab-treated patients. Elevated AST occurred in 44% of patients, with Grade 3 or 4 AST elevation occurring in 3.2%. Elevated bilirubin occurred in 15% of patients, with Grade 3 or 4 total bilirubin elevations occurring in 1.6% of patients.
L1519: Hypersensitivity was also added to the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI as a potential risk. Clinical signs and symptoms of hypersensitivity may include, but are not limited to, rash and bronchospasm. There were 4 patients identified with hypersensitivity adverse events in the review of overall safety data (3 patients in the 100 mg dose group and 1 patient in the 10 mg dose group). The first patient received 10 mg on Cycle 4 Day 1 and 8 days later developed Grade 3 bronchospasm attributed to a mucous plug. The event resolved and no action was taken with tarlatamab. The second patient received 100 mg on Cycle 2 Day 15 and was subsequently hospitalized for maculopapular rash and treated with prednisolone, zeroven, and betamethasone valerate. The rash resolved and no action was taken with
L1520: tarlatamab. The third patient received his last dose of 100 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and 8 days later experienced symptoms such as cough, hemoptysis, tachycardia, desaturation at 65% and tachypnea at 35 respirations per minute. Despite oxygenation, the patient's general condition deteriorated rapidly. A life-threatening event of respiratory distress was diagnosed, and at the time of the event the patient died from progressive disease. The last patient experienced dyspnea, bronchospasm, and low oxygen saturation approximately 35 minutes after receiving Cycle 1 Day 8 dose of 100 mg, which was the last dose of tarlatamab. Symptoms resolved within 1 hour of receiving antihistamines and steroids.

Grade \ge 3 and serious adverse events were reported for 63.0% and 59.3% of subjects, respectively. Fatal adverse events were reported for 19 subjects (4.2%), which were treatment related for 2 subjects (0.4%). Few subjects (7.0%) discontinued tarlatamab due to adverse events.

L1555: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1556: In the monotherapy studies, the subject incidence of adverse events, grade 3 and higher adverse events, and adverse events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab were comparable between the 10 and 100 mg doses. The incidences of serious adverse events and adverse events leading to dose interruption or reduction were slightly lower for subjects treated with 10 mg compared with subjects treated with 100 mg (Table 53).
L1557: Adverse events that have been determined by the Sponsor to be associated with tarlatamab, based on the totality of the data, are considered to be adverse drug reactions. The identified adverse drug reactions are anemia, neutropenia, constipation, nausea, pyrexia, asthenia, fatigue, CRS, neutrophil count decreased, decreased appetite, hyponatremia, dysgeusia, ICANS, neurotoxicity, tremor, confusional state, delirium, and dyspnea.
L1558: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1559: FDA's analysis of treatment emergent adverse events in the pooled safety population is provided in Table 13.
L1560: The most common (&gt;20%) adverse reactions were cytokine release syndrome (55%), fatigue (51%), dysgeusia (36%), pyrexia (36%), decreased appetite (34%), constipation (30%), musculoskeletal pain (30%), anemia (27%) and nausea (22%).
L1561: A total of 393 previously unreported adverse events were identified in 111 patients following 100% SDR/SDV of adverse events in Study 20200491. As a result of the revised 90-day update safety analysis, twenty-eight additional treatment-emergent adverse events were identified in 20 patients treated with tarlatamab at the 10 mg dose. Out of 28 adverse events, 14 Grade 3 adverse events were reported for 10 patients (7.5%). This included lymphopenia (n=4), general health deterioration (n=2), activated partial thromboplastin time prolonged, acute kidney injury, anemia, back pain, cholestasis, decreased appetite, hyperglycemia, and hyponatremia (n=1 in 1 patient each). Two serious adverse events of anemia (one Grade 3 and one Grade 2) were reported for 1 patient treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at the recommended dose of 10 mg.
L1562: The overall incidence of any grade CRS increased from 101 patients (54%) to 103 patients (55%) in the 10 mg dose group with no changes in ≥Grade 3 CRS events. The 2 new CRS events in the 10 mg dose group were both Grade 1 and nonserious events. There were no changes in ≥ Grade 2 CRS events, serious CRS events, or CRS events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab. There were 2 new cases of ICANS, both were Grade 1 events. Overall, there were no changes in ≥Grade 2 ICANS events, serious ICANS, or ICANS events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab. In addition, there were 6 new cases of neurologic toxicities, peripheral sensory neuropathy, leukoencephalopathy, insomnia (n=2 each), muscular weakness, leukoencephalopathy and dysgeusia (n=1 each), which were all low grade (grade 1 or 2).
L1563: Overall, there was no change in ≥Grade 3 neurological events, serious events, or events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab.

L1571: ## Laboratory Findings
L1572: ## Data:
L1573: In general, clinical laboratory evaluations (eg, serum chemistry, hematology) demonstrated no clinically significant effect of tarlatamab (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4).
L1574: The laboratory parameters with a \ge 3 grade change from baseline reported for \ge 5\% of subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were decrease in lymphocytes (24.3%) and decrease in sodium (12.7%). A lower incidence of grade \ge 3 decreases from baseline in sodium was observed for subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg compared with 100 mg (8.8% vs 16.0%) (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4, SCS). Adverse events associated with neutropenia are discussed in Section 8.2.5.3. Neutropenia.
L1575: The incidences of liver function test abnormalities were low across all studies. Increases in AST and ALT were mainly grade 1 and grade 2 and occurred in the setting of CRS (see Section 8.2.5.1. Cytokine Release Syndrome). There were no cases of drug-induced liver injury.
L1576: ## Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
L1577: Overall, the subject incidence and preferred terms of adverse events related to laboratory abnormalities and changes from baseline in laboratory parameters in subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were consistent with the initial ISS analysis. Laboratory parameters with \ge 3 grades change from baseline reported for \ge 5\% of subjects treated with monotherapy were decrease in lymphocytes from grade 0 to 3 (19.0%) and decrease in sodium from grade 0 to 3 (11.8%), consistent with the initial ISS analysis. No new cases that met the Hy's Law criteria were reported since the initial ISS analysis.
L1578: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1579: There were no notable trends in laboratory evaluations, and the results were consistent with the known safety profile of tarlatamab (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4 Summary of Clinical Safety).
L1580: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1581: FDA's analysis of selected laboratory abnormalities that worsened from baseline in the pooled safety population are provided in Table 39 below.

L1593: FDA agrees with the Applicant's analysis of vital signs.
L1594: ## **Electrocardiograms (ECGs)**
L1595: ## **Data:**
L1596: Electrocardiogram (ECG) results are for Study 20200491 and 20160323. Overall, elevated postbaseline corrected QT intervals (Fridericia) (QTcFs) were reported infrequently in subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy, with maximum postbaseline QTcF intervals &gt; 480 to 500 msec or &gt; 500 msec reported for 2 subjects (0.4%) each. No subject with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg had postbaseline QTcF intervals &gt; 480 to 500 msec or &gt; 500 msec. No subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg or 100 mg had postbaseline QTcF intervals &gt; 500 msec.
L1597: Few subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy had changes from baseline in QTcF interval &gt; 30 to 60 msec (7.4%) or &gt; 60 msec (0.9%) with similar incidences between the 10 mg and 100 mg groups (Section 4.2 of Module 2.7.4, SCS).
L1598: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1599: Overall, the incidence of changes in ECGs was low and no notable ECG abnormalities potentially associated with tarlatamab were observed.
L1600: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1601: FDA agrees with the Applicant's description of the incidence and severity of QT prolongation. No clinically significant QT prolongation was observed in the clinical and non-clinical QT assessment. The data presented do not suggest a mechanism for QT prolongation by tarlatamab.
L1602: ## **Immunogenicity**
L1603: ## **Data:**
L1604: To support this marketing application, immunogenicity was determined by measuring antitarlatamab antibody formation using a validated electro-chemiluminescence-based bridging immunoassay in 5 clinical studies (20160323, 20200491, [REDACTED](b) (4)). For the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, samples confirmed to be positive for binding antibodies were subsequently tested in a validated cell-based bioassay to determine the neutralizing activity against tarlatamab. Detailed description of these assays and impact analyses is presented in the Integrated Summary of Immunogenicity (Module 5.3.5.3).
L1605: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L1606: At the proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W, treatment-emergent antitarlatamab incidence was 12% (3 of 25 subjects) for the FIH Study 20160323 and 3.2% (4 of 124 subjects) for the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, for a pooled total of 7 of 149 subjects (4.7%) across these studies (ISS Table 14-10.1.1). In the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, which employed the neutralizing assay, none of the subjects receiving the 10 mg target dose developed neutralizing antibodies. Potential impact of antitarlatamab antibodies on the PK, clinical efficacy, and clinical safety of tarlatamab was assessed with the following results:
L1607: - Based on the population PK model, individuals with positive ADA binding status at any time during the study were estimated to have approximately 21% higher clearance, which translated to an approximately 24% decrease in serum trough concentrations on average (see Sections 6.3.1 and 19.4.1).
- The efficacy results (including ORR, DCR, and DOR) were comparable for antitarlatamab binding antibody positive subjects compared with those who were antitarlatamab antibody negative (see Section 6.3.1).
- There was no impact of antitarlatamab antibody development on safety based on assessment of adverse events (see Section 3.3 of Module 2.7.4, SCS).
L1608: ## *Safety Update as of 02 October 2023*
L1609: Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the overall incidence of antitarlatamab antibody development was low. Overall, 44 subjects (9.4%) treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were antitarlatamab antibody positive (23 pre-existing antitarlatamab antibody positive and 21 treatment-induced antitarlatamab antibody positive) out of (b)(4) subjects with an on-study result across Studies 20160323, 20200491, and 20200040 (90-daySU ISS Table 14-10.1.1). These included (b)(4) subjects treated with 10 mg tarlatamab (b)(4) pre-existing antitarlatamab antibody positive and (b)(4) treatment induced antitarlatamab antibody positive) out of (b)(4) subjects with an on-study result and (b)(4) subjects treated with 100 mg tarlatamab (b)(4) pre-existing antitarlatamab antibody positive and 5 treatment induced antitarlatamab antibody positive) out of 163 subjects with an on-study result from Studies 20160323 and 20200491.
L1610: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1611: The incidence of antitarlatamab antibody development is low. At the proposed clinical regimen of 10 mg Q2W, 7/149 (4.7%) of tarlatamab-treated subjects developed treatment-emergent antitarlatamab antibodies. The majority of these subjects had low magnitude antibody response and none of the subjects who were ADA positive developed neutralizing antibodies. The development of antitarlatamab antibodies did not have a clinically relevant impact on tarlatamab efficacy or safety.
L1612: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1613: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment that the incidence of ADA was low. In Study
L1614: 20200491 in the 1mg step up/10mg dosage cohort, 3.2% (4/124) of patients tested positive for ADA and none of the patients developed Nab. Due to the low occurrence of ADA, the impact of ADA on PK, safety, and effectiveness is inconclusive.
L1615: ## 8.2.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1616: ## 8.2.5.1. Cytokine Release Syndrome
L1617: ## Data:
L1618: ## *Monotherapy Studies*
L1619: Using the AMQ narrow search, CRS adverse events of any grade were reported for 270 subjects (59.1%) across all doses (ISS Table 14-6.1.7), with 265 subjects having resolved events and 6 subjects with ongoing events as of the data cutoff date (ISS Table 14-6.7.5). Serious CRS events were reported for 122 subjects (26.7%), and 3 subjects (0.7%) had a CRS event that led to tarlatamab treatment discontinuation.
L1620: In subjects receiving tarlatamab 10 mg monotherapy in the 2 clinical studies, using the AMQ narrow search, CRS occurred in 53.8% of subjects, including (worst) grade 1 in 31.0%, grade 2 in 21.4%, and grade 4 in 0.8% of subjects. No subject had a grade 3 or grade 5 event of CRS. Serious events of CRS were reported in 23.1% of subjects. There were no fatal CRS events.
L1621: After the first dose of tarlatamab (cycle 1 day 1), 41.3% of subjects experienced any grade CRS, with 28.8% of subjects experiencing any grade CRS after the second dose (ISS Table 14-6.2.404). The majority of CRS events occurred after the first 2 doses, with 8.8% of subjects experiencing CRS following the third dose or later. Following the day 1 infusion, 15.6% of subjects experienced \ge grade 2 CRS. Following the day 8 infusion, 4.4% of subjects experienced \ge grade 2 CRS. Clinical signs and symptoms of CRS included pyrexia, hypotension, fatigue, tachycardia, headache, chills, nausea, and vomiting. The majority of these events did not lead to tarlatamab discontinuation.
L1622: Subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 100 mg had higher rates of grade \ge 3 CRS events (6.1%) compared with subjects treated with 10 mg (1.3%) (ISS Table 14-6.1.7). Otherwise, the overall frequency and severity of CRS events (ie, grade 2 and above, serious CRS, and CRS events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab) were generally similar among subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 and 100 mg. Hence, the median time to onset and duration of CRS are provided below for the overall pooled monotherapy studies with results for
L1623: 10 and 100 mg provided in Section 2.1.5.3.1.1 of Module 2.7.4, SCS.
L1624: The median time to first onset of CRS (AMQ, narrow search) from the first dose of tarlatamab was 19.8 hours for any grade CRS, 29.0 hours for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 56.1 hours for grade \ge 3 CRS. The median time to first onset of CRS (AMQ, narrow search) from the last prior dose of tarlatamab was 12.6 hours for any grade CRS, 15.4 hours for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 32.0 hours for grade \ge 3 CRS (ISS Table 14-6.7.1 and ISS Table 14-6.7.3).
L1625: The median duration of resolved CRS adverse events (AMQ, narrow search) was 3 days for any grade CRS, 4 days for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 4 days for grade \ge 3 CRS, as of the data cutoff date. The Kaplan-Meier median (95% CI) time to resolution was 4 (3, 4) days for any grade CRS, 4 (3, 4) days for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 4 (3, 5) days for grade \ge 3 CRS (ISS Table 14-6.7.5).
L1626: ## Study 20200491
L1627: To provide the clinically most meaningful safety analysis, the following CRS analysis focuses on CRS events in Study 20200491 reported for subjects treated with tarlatamab 10 mg with a 1 mg step dose in cycle 1 where the data collected were more detailed for CRS. When meaningful, data are also provided for subjects in the 100 mg group initiated by step dosing in cycle 1, since the step dose of 1 mg is common to both treatment groups and will be used with the dose of 10 mg.
L1628: In Study 20200491, the incidence of CRS adverse events for subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg and 100 mg was 40.6% and 28.7% on cycle 1 day 1; 29.3% and 49.4% on cycle 1 day 8; 7.5% and 6.9% on cycle 1 day 15. The incidence of CRS events was lower in cycle 2 and beyond, ranging from 0 to 1.5% in the 10 mg group and 0 to 4.6% in the 100 mg group (Table 14-6.5.401 of Study 20200491).
L1629: On cycle 1 day 1, after the 1 mg infusion, 31 of 220 subjects (14.1%) had a grade \ge 2 CRS event that generally required interventions of low flow oxygen and IV hydration (Table 14-6.13.402 of Study 20200491). On cycle 1 day 1, grade \ge 2 CRS events that required interventions including low and high flow oxygen or IV hydration occurred before 24 hours in 16 of 220 subjects (7.3%), and after 24 hours in 4 of 220 subjects (1.8%). The median time to first onset of grade \ge 2 CRS (AMQ, narrow search) was 25.9 hours from the first dose of tarlatamab (Table 14-6.10.2 of Study 20200491).
L1630: On cycle 1 day 8, in the 10 mg group, there were 4 of 133 subjects (3%) that had grade \ge 2 CRS events that generally required interventions of low flow oxygen and IV hydration. No grade \ge 3 CRS events were reported (Table 14-6.5.401 of Study 20200491). High flow oxygen (\ge 6 L/min) was not used in events after the cycle 1 day 8 dose (Figure 14-6.1.401 of Study 20200491). No grade \ge 2 CRS events that required interventions including low flow oxygen and IV hydration
L1631: occurred before 24 hours (Table 14.6.13.401 of Study 20200491). One subject in the 10 mg cohort had grade 2 CRS that required IV hydration and a single vasopressor at 69.8 hours after the cycle 1 day 8 dose. The median time to grade 1 CRS was 8.2 hours, and the median time from grade 1 to grade 2 was 20.2 hours from the first dose of tarlatamab (Table 14-6.6.401 of Study 20200491). The median (Q1, Q3) time to first onset of grade \ge 2 CRS (AMQ, narrow search) was 15.2 (9.0, 31.2) hours from the last prior dose of tarlatamab (Table 14-6.10.5 of Study 20200491).
L1632: The most common treatments administered for any grade CRS were low flow oxygen (for hypoxia), IV fluids (for hypotension), and tocilizumab. Additional less common treatments included high flow oxygen (for hypoxia), and vasopressors (for hypotension) (Table 14-6.13.501 of Study 20200491).
L1633: ## Combination Therapy Studies
L1634: Across the combination studies, the incidence of CRS using the AMQ narrow search was 29 of 54 subjects (53.7%), and the most frequently reported preferred term was CRS. Serious CRS events were reported for 11 subjects (20.3%) and grade \ge 3 events occurred in 1 subject (1.8%). No events were fatal. See Section 2.1.5.3.2.1 of Module 2.7.4 (SCS) for additional information.
L1635: ## Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
L1636: ## Monotherapy Studies
L1637: Of the (b) (4) subjects overall in the Monotherapy Safety Analysis Set, (b) (4) had CRS adverse events of any grade using the AMQ broad search. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported (\ge 20% of subjects) preferred terms included CRS (60.7%), pyrexia (39.0%), fatigue (29.3%), nausea (25.4%), and asthenia (21.5%).
L1638: Using the AMQ narrow search, CRS adverse events of any grade were reported for 294 subjects (60.7%), with 286 subjects having resolved events and 9 subjects with ongoing events as of the data cutoff date (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.7.6); CRS was the only preferred term reported. As reported in the initial ISS analysis, a grade 4 CRS event was reported for 1 subject (0.2%) treated with 10 mg tarlatamab monotherapy. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, no CRS events were fatal (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.1.9).
L1639: The median (Q1, Q3) time to first onset of CRS (AMQ narrow search) from the first dose of tarlatamab was 18.9 (8.8, 175.3) hours for any grade CRS, 26.0 (9.3, 188.5) hours for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 55.3 (22.8, 196.6) hours for grade \ge 3 CRS (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.7.1).
L1640: The median (Q1, Q3) duration of resolved CRS (AMQ narrow search) adverse events was 3 (2, 5) days for any grade CRS, 4 (2, 6) days for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 4 (3, 5) days for grade \ge 3 CRS (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.7.6). The Kaplan-Meier median (95% CI) time to resolution was 3 (3, 4)
L1641: days for any grade CRS, 4 (3, 4) days for grade \ge 2 CRS, and 4 (3, 5) days for grade \ge 3 CRS (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.7.6).
L1642: Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, subjects treated with tarlatamab 100 mg had higher rates of serious and grade \ge 3 CRS events compared with subjects treated with tarlatamab 10 mg. Otherwise, the clinical presentation of the CRS events was generally similar among subjects treated with 10 mg and 100 mg.
L1643: ## Combination Therapy Studies
L1644: Across the combination studies, the incidence of CRS using the AMQ narrow search was 52 of 109 subjects (47.7%), and the most frequently reported preferred term was CRS. Serious CRS events were reported for 21 subjects (19.3%) and grade \ge 3 events occurred in 1 subject (0.9%). No events were fatal. See Section 2.1.5.3.1.2 of 90-Day Safety Update for additional information.
L1645: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1646: Most cases of CRS were grade 1 and grade 2 events that could be conservatively managed. Grade 3 and grade 4 CRS adverse events were rare. Most cases did not require measures such as tocilizumab, oxygen, or vasopressors. In general, events of CRS did not recur after subsequent doses of tarlatamab. In cases where it did progress, it took many hours to evolve from grade 1 to grade 2 to grade 3, where intervention would be required; the median time to first onset of CRS (AMQ, narrow search) from the first dose of tarlatamab was 26.0 and 55.3 hours for grade \ge 2 and grade \ge 3 CRS, and from the last prior dose of tarlatamab was 15.1 and 30.6 hours, respectively. There were few grade 2 and higher events or hospital-based interventions used from cycle 1 day 15 and beyond.
L1647: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1648: FDA agrees that most instances of CRS were Grade 1 or 2 and that most patients experienced CRS following tarlatamab administration of initial step up dose of 1 mg IV on Cycle 1 Day 1. Patients received premedication with dexamethasone 8 mg IV administered 1 hour prior to infusion on Days 1 and 8 of Cycle 1 only and post IV hydration with 1 liter of normal saline over 4-5 hours.
L1649: In the pooled safety population (n = 187), CRS occurred in 55% of patients, including 34% Grade 1, 19% Grade 2, 1.1% Grade 3 and 0.5% Grade 4 of tarlatamab-treated patients. Tocilizumab was used in 9% of patients and there were no fatal Grade 5 events. Recurrent CRS occurred in 24% of tarlatamab-treated patients (18% Grade 1, 6% Grade 2).
L1650: Most events (43%) of CRS occurred after the first dose with 29% of patients experiencing any grade CRS after the second dose and 9% of patients experiencing CRS following the third dose or later. Following the Day 1, Day 8, Day 15 infusions, 16%, 4.3%, and 2.1% of patients
L1651: experienced \ge Grade 2 CRS, respectively. The median time to onset of all grade CRS from most recent dose of tarlatamab was 13.5 hours (range: 1 to 268 hours). The median time to onset of \ge Grade 2 CRS from most recent dose of tarlatamab was 14.6 hours (range: 2 to 566 hours).
L1652: FDA did not agree with the Applicant's initial proposal in the USPI to monitor patients for (b) (4) hours after first infusion and to remain within 1 hour of an appropriate healthcare setting for (b) (4) hours after each tarlatamab infusion on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8. In Part 3 of the pivotal study (modified monitoring cohort), patients were hospitalized for 24 hours post tarlatamab infusion for Cycle 1 day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 doses. Based on the data provided, FDA agreed that monitoring of patients from the start of tarlatamab infusion for 22-24 hours on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 in an appropriate healthcare setting would be appropriate to mitigate risk. FDA also recommend patients remain within 1 hour of an appropriate healthcare setting for a total of 48 hours from the start of the infusion with tarlatamab following Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8, accompanied by a caregiver.
L1653: FDA also carefully considered the need for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) given the major identified safety finding of CRS and have determined that a REMS is not needed. Based on our review, we have determined that the toxicity profile of the tarlatamab can be adequately conveyed through labeling (including a Boxed Warning for serious or life-threatening CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS). In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks. Refer to Section 12 for rationale for not requiring a REMS.
L1654: ## 8.2.5.2. Immune Effector Cell-associated Neurotoxicity Syndrome
L1655: ## Data:
L1656: In the monotherapy studies, a total of 72 (b) (4) had ICANS and associated neurological events (AMQ broad search) with a lower incidence in the 10 mg group (15 subjects [9.4%]) than in the 100 mg group (35 subjects [21.5%]). Serious ICANS events occurred in 27 subjects (5.9%) overall with a lower incidence with 10 mg (4 subjects [2.5%]) than with 100 mg (18 subjects [11.0%]). Fourteen subjects (3.1%) had grade \ge 3 ICANS events (none in the 10 mg group), of whom 2 subjects (0.4%) had grade \ge 4 events, and there were no fatal events (ISS Table 14-6.1.7).
L1657: ICANS events led to dose interruption in 11 subjects (2.4%), mainly from the 100 mg group (9 [5.5%] vs 1 [0.6%] from the 10 mg group) (ISS Table 14-6.1.7). ICANS events led to treatment discontinuation in 6 subjects (1.3%), mainly from the 100 mg group (4 subjects [2.5%] vs 1 [0.6%] from 10 mg).
L1658: In the 10 mg dose group (N = 160), 15 subjects (9.4%) experienced ICANS and associated neurological events. Preferred terms in these subjects included ICANS (7 subjects), assessed as
L1659: related to tarlatamab in 4 subjects (2.5%); muscular weakness (7 subjects), aphasia (1 subject), depressed level of consciousness (1 subject), and leukoencephalopathy (1 subject). Four subjects (2.5%) had serious events, all ICANS by preferred term. The median time from the last dose of tarlatamab to the first onset of ICANS was 3 days (range: 1 to 15 days). The median time to resolution of ICANS was 33 days (range 1 to 93 days).
L1660: In the 100 mg dose group (N = 163), 35 subjects (21.5%) had ICANS events, with serious events reported in 18 subjects (11.0%). The preferred terms for the serious events were: ICANS (10 [6.1%]) and encephalopathy (6 [3.7%]), all assessed as related to tarlatamab. The remaining preferred terms were low incidence, mostly nonserious and not related to tarlatamab (ISS Table 14-6.2.67).
L1661: (b) (4)
L1662: ## Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
L1663: Since the initial ISS analysis, 2 additional subjects had ICANS and associated neurological events based on the AMQ broad search; 74 of the (b) (4) subjects overall in the Monotherapy Safety Analysis Set had ICANS and associated neurological events, which were considered related to tarlatamab in 56 subjects (11.6%). As reported in the initial ISS analysis, 2 subjects (0.4%) had grade \ge 4 events. Serious ICANS and associated neurological events were reported for 29 subjects (6.0%), and 6 subjects (1.2%) had events leading to tarlatamab discontinuation; no ICANS and associated neurological events were fatal (Table 16 and 90-daySU ISS Table 14 6.1.9). Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported (\ge 2% of subjects) preferred terms were muscular weakness (5.0%), ICANS (4.3%), and encephalopathy (2.5%).
L1664: Among subjects receiving 10 mg tarlatamab, since the initial ISS analysis, a serious event of encephalopathy was reported in 1 subject (0.5%) which was considered to be related to tarlatamab by the investigator. The subject was known to have brain metastases; details are provided in Section 2.1.5.3.2.1 of 90-Day Safety Update.
L1665: (b) (4)
L1666: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1667: ICANS is an important identified risk for tarlatamab. In the tarlatamab monotherapy studies (n = 457), ICANS and associated neurological adverse events using the ICANS AMQ broad search strategy were observed in 72 subjects (15.8%). At the 10 mg tarlatamab dose (n = 160), ICANS events were observed in 15 subjects (9.4%). Across the tarlatamab monotherapy studies, most ICANS events were mild to moderate. No fatal events have been reported. Most of the events were self-limiting without the need for treatment discontinuation or dose reduction.
L1668: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1669: Based on FDA analysis in the pooled safety population, neurologic toxicity including ICANS occurred in 47% of patients who received tarlatamab, including 10% Grade 3; there were no Grade 4 events. The most frequent neurologic toxicities were headache (14%), peripheral neuropathy (7%), dizziness (7%), insomnia (6%), muscular weakness (3.7%), delirium (2.1%), syncope (1.6%) and neurotoxicity (1.1%).
L1670: ICANS occurred in 9% of patients and recurrent ICANS occurred in 1.6% of patients. Most patients experienced ICANS following cycle 2 day 1 (24%). Following Day 1, Day 8, and Day 15 infusions, 0.5%, 0.5% and 3.7% of patients experienced \ge Grade 2 ICANS, respectively. The median time to onset of ICANS from the first dose of tarlatamab was 29.5 days (range: 1 to 154 days). The median time to resolution of ICANS was 33 days (range: 1 to 93 days).
L1671: FDA did not agree with the Applicant's initial proposal in the USPI to monitor patients for (b) (4) hours after first infusion and to remain within 1 hour of an appropriate healthcare setting for (b) (4) hours after each tarlatamab infusion on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8. In Part 3 of the pivotal study (modified monitoring cohort), patients were hospitalized for 24 hours post tarlatamab infusion for Cycle 1 day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 doses. Based on the data provided, FDA agreed that monitoring of patients from the start of tarlatamab infusion for 22-24 hours on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 in an appropriate healthcare setting would be appropriate to mitigate risk. FDA also recommend patients remain within 1 hour of an appropriate healthcare setting for a total of 48 hours from the start of the infusion with tarlatamab following Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8, accompanied by a caregiver.
L1672: FDA carefully considered the need for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) given the major identified safety findings of neurologic toxicity including ICANS and have determined that a REMS is not needed. Based on our review, we have determined that the toxicity profile of the tarlatamab can be adequately conveyed through labeling (including a Boxed Warning for serious or life-threatening CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS). In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks. Refer to Section 12 for rationale for not requiring a REMS.

L1680: ## 8.2.5.3. Neutropenia
L1681: ## Data:
L1682: Across the monotherapy studies (b) (4) subjects (b) (4) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search (ISS Table 14-6.1.7). Using this search, the most frequently reported (\ge 5\% of subjects) preferred terms were neutropenia (7.9%), decreased neutrophil count (6.1%), and decreased white blood cell count (5.3%) (ISS Table 14-6.2.65). Using the narrow search, 63 subjects (13.8%) had adverse events of neutropenia, 37 subjects (8.1%) had grade \ge 3 events, and 12 subjects (2.6%) had grade \ge 4 events. Serious neutropenia events were reported for 9 subjects (2.0%), and 2 subjects (0.4%) had neutropenia events leading to tarlatamab discontinuation. No neutropenia adverse events were fatal. Results for the 10 and 100 mg dose groups were similar for neutropenia, with the exception of a higher incidence of grade \ge 3 events with 100 mg (11.7%) than with 10 mg (6.3%).
L1683: Based on pooled safety data from Studies 20200491 and 20160323, in the 10 mg dose group, 27 of 160 subjects (16.9%) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search (ISS Table 14-6.1.7) with neutropenia (8.8%) and decreased neutrophil count (5.0%) reported in \ge 5\% of subjects (ISS Table 14-6.2.25). Based on the AMQ narrow search, 23 subjects (14.4%) had any event of neutropenia, 10 subjects (6.3%) had grade \ge 3 events, and 4 subjects (2.5%) had grade \ge 4 events; serious neutropenia events were reported for 2 subjects (1.3%) and are described in Section 2.1.5.3.4.1 of Module 2.7.4, SCS. No neutropenia events were fatal. Neutropenia leading to dose interruption occurred in 0.6% of subjects with none leading to treatment discontinuation. The events were mainly nonserious and resolved spontaneously or with the use of granulocyte colony stimulating factor (G-CSF).
L1684: The median time from the first dose of tarlatamab to the first onset of any grade and grade \ge 2 neutropenia events was 43 and 45 days, respectively (ISS Table 14-6.7.1). The median time from the last prior dose of tarlatamab to the first onset of any grade and grade \ge 2 neutropenia events was 8 and 13 days, respectively. The median duration of resolved grade \ge 2 neutropenia adverse events was 8 days, with 17 subjects having resolved events and 2 subjects with ongoing events as of the data cutoff date. The Kaplan-Meier median (95% CI) time to resolution was 8 (6, 14) days (ISS Table 14-6.7.5). Overall, in the 10 mg cohort, 3 (1.9%) subjects received G-CSF treatment for the neutropenia events.
L1685: In the 100 mg dose group, 30 of 163 subjects (18.4%) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search (ISS Table 14-6.1.7) with decreased neutrophil count (8.6%), neutropenia (8.0%), and decreased white blood cell count (8.0%), reported in \ge 5\% of subjects (ISS Table 14-6.2.25). Based on the AMQ narrow search, 27 subjects (16.6%) had adverse events of neutropenia, 19 subjects (11.7%) had grade \ge 3 events, and 6 subjects (3.7%) had grade \ge 4 events; serious neutropenia events were reported for 4 subjects (2.5%); 2 subjects (1.2%) had events that led to discontinuation of tarlatamab; and no neutropenia events were fatal. The median time from the first dose of tarlatamab to the first onset of any grade and grade \ge 2 neutropenia events, from last prior dose of tarlatamab to onset of events, median duration of resolved events, and median time to resolution were similar to those seen with 10 mg (ISS Table 14-6.7.5). Overall, in the 100 mg cohorts, 7 subjects (4.4%) were treated with G-CSF for their neutropenia events.
L1686: ## Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
L1687: Of the (b) (4) subjects overall in the Monotherapy Safety Analysis Set, (b) (4) subjects (b) (4) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search (90-daySU ISS Table 14-6.1.9). Using this search, consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported preferred terms were neutropenia (8.5%), decreased neutrophil count (6.4%), and decreased white blood cell count (4.8%). Using the narrow search, 70 subjects (14.5%) had adverse events of
L1688: neutropenia. A total of 61 (12.6%) had grade \ge 2 neutropenia events and 39 subjects (8.1%) had grade \ge 3 events. Serious neutropenia events were reported for 11 subjects (2.3%) and 2 subjects (0.4%) had neutropenia adverse events leading to tarlatamab discontinuation. No neutropenia adverse events were fatal.
L1689: Neutropenia results in both the 10 mg and 100 mg target dose groups of tarlatamab remained consistent with the initial ISS analysis, with similar frequency, severity and types of neutropenia events (Section 2.1.5.3.4 90-Day Safety Update). In subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg, 32 of 187 subjects (17.1%) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search. Based on the AMQ narrow search, 28 subjects (15.0%) had adverse events of neutropenia. In subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 100 mg, 31 of 163 subjects (19.0%) had adverse events of neutropenia based on the AMQ broad search; based on the AMQ narrow search, 29 subjects (17.8%) had adverse events of neutropenia. The proportion of subjects requiring treatment with G-CSF was similar to the initial ISS analysis (2.1% and 4.4% for 10 and 100 mg, respectively).
L1690: As of 02 October 2023, a serious adverse event of neutrophil count decreased was reported for 1 (1.3%) additional subject enrolled in Study 20200491 (10 mg). Details are provided in Section 2.1.5.3.4.1 of 90-Day Safety Update.
L1691: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1692: Based on the available safety data from clinical studies, neutropenia is a risk of tarlatamab. Neutropenia observed with tarlatamab is generally mild and not associated with febrile neutropenia. The majority of neutropenia events were non-serious and resolved spontaneously with or without the use of G-CSF. The data do not suggest that tarlatamab causes immunosuppression and therefore the safety profile is not anticipated to be different in this patient population.
L1693: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1694: FDA's analysis of treatment emergent adverse events of interest the pooled safety population included cytopenias which includes neutropenia, thrombocytopenia and anemia. Refer to Table 56 for laboratory findings including cytopenia.
L1695: In the pooled safety population, decreased neutrophils occurred in 12% of patients, including Grade 3 or 4 in 6% of patients. The median time to onset for Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia was 29.5 days (range: 2 to 213). Decreased platelets occurred in 33% including Grade 3 or 4 in 3.2% of patients. The median time to onset for Grade 3 or 4 decreased platelets was 50 days (range: 3 to 420). Decreased hemoglobin occurred in 58% including 5% Grade 3 or 4. Febrile neutropenia occurred in 0.5% of patients treated with IMDELLTRA.

L1703: ## 8.2.7. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L1704: ## **Data:**
L1705: Summaries of adverse events and treatment-related adverse events; subject incidence of adverse events and treatment-related adverse events by preferred term; and subject incidence of adverse events and treatment-related adverse events by system organ class were analyzed by intrinsic and extrinsic factor subgroups of race (American Indian or Alaska Native, Asian, Black or African American, Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander, White, Other), ethnicity (Hispanic/Latino, Not Hispanic/Latino – only for subjects reporting 'White' race), age (&lt; 65 years versus ≥ 65 years and &lt; 75 years versus ≥ 75 years), sex, ECOG status (ECOG status 0, 1, 2), and region (Asia, Europe, North America, rest of world).
L1706: Overall summaries of adverse events by subgroup are provided in Section 5.1 and Section 5.2 of Module 2.7.4, SCS. In the integrated monotherapy analysis set, no notable safety trends were observed across the subgroups examined. No meaningful differences were observed in the types of adverse events or treatment-related adverse events reported in the subgroups examined; however, small sample sizes in some subgroups limit the ability to draw meaningful conclusions.
L1707: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1708: Overall, subgroup analyses demonstrated a pattern of events consistent with that reported for the overall study population.

The rate of AEs and SAEs were relatively similar across all categories including in patients with ECOG of 0 (n=56) and ECOG of 1 (n=129). In patients with ECOG 0 the incidences of AEs and SAEs were 100% and 48%. In patients with ECOG 1 the incidences of AEs were 99% and 58% respectively.

L1730: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
## **Data:**
L1731: No clinical studies of tarlatamab have been conducted in pregnant or breastfeeding women. No breastfeeding cases were reported with exposure to tarlatamab.
L1732: There are no clinical studies to evaluate the effect of tarlatamab on fertility.
L1733: **The Applicant's Position:**
L1734: There are no available data from the use of tarlatamab in pregnant women. Based on its mechanism of action, tarlatamab may cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman.
L1735: It is unknown whether tarlatamab is secreted in human milk. Because many medicinal products, including antibodies, can be secreted in human milk, a risk to the newborns/infants cannot be excluded. A decision must be made whether to discontinue breast-feeding or to discontinue tarlatamab treatment taking into account the benefit of breast-feeding for the child and the benefit of tarlatamab treatment for the woman.
L1736: There are no clinical studies to evaluate the effect of tarlatamab on fertility.
L1737: **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1738: FDA agrees with the Applicant's statement. There were no patients who were discontinued from trial due to pregnancy, and birth control measures during treatment and ongoing pregnancy screening were enforced to ensure that no fetus was exposed to study drug. Therefore, no information on the use of tarlatamab during pregnancy or lactation. Refer to Section 5.5.4 for Pharmacology Toxicology review of reproductive and developmental toxicology.
L1739: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth

This postmarketing requirement is intended to provide additional safety information to support the safety of the product. Please refer to Section 13 for postmarketing requirements.
L1769: ## 8.2.11. **Integrated Assessment of Safety**
L1770: ## **Data:**
L1771: The key risks with tarlatamab treatment are as follows:
L1772: ## *Cytokine release syndrome*
L1773: - Cytokine release syndrome events were reported in approximately half of the subjects who received the 10 mg dose of tarlatamab across monotherapy studies. Most of the CRS events were nonserious and mild or moderate. No fatal CRS events have been reported. In general, CRS is manageable and reversible (interventions may include oxygen supplementation, IV fluids, vasopressors, and/or anti-interleukin [IL]-6 therapy). In some instances, tarlatamab treatment interruption or discontinuation may be required for reversibility.
L1774: ## *Immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS)*
L1775: - Across monotherapy studies, ICANS and associated neurotoxicity events have been reported in &lt; 10% of subjects who received the 10 mg dose of tarlatamab. Most of the events were mild or moderate. No fatal events have been reported. Most of the events were self-limiting without the need for treatment discontinuation or dose reduction.
L1776: These risks can be successfully managed by monitoring patients for signs and symptoms of CRS and ICANS following tarlatamab infusion, with temporary interruption or discontinuation depending on the severity of the event.
L1777: Tarlatamab was generally well tolerated, with a low rate of adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation in pivotal Study 20200491 and FIH Study 20160323 (6.9% in the 10 mg dose group).
L1778: Routine risk minimization activities (ie, risk communications through prescribing information or product packaging) are considered adequate to address the safety concerns associated with the use of tarlatamab.
L1779: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1780: Tarlatamab is generally well tolerated, and risks associated with tarlatamab use can be adequately managed through communication in product labeling, routine pharmacovigilance activities, and routine risk minimization activities. Tarlatamab demonstrated an acceptable safety profile in adult subjects with previously treated advanced SCLC.
L1781: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1782: FDA agrees that the safety data presented in the BLA are derived from the pooled safety population from Study 20200491 and Study 20160323 in 187 patients with extensive stage SCLC
L1783: who were treated with tarlatamab at a step dose of 1 mg on Cycle Day 1 followed by the proposed dose of 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, Cycle 1 Day 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter. FDA agrees that the CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS can be managed through communication in labeling (including a black box warning). In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks. Other significant adverse events included infections, hepatotoxicity and hypersensitivity which are included in the Warnings and Precaution section of the label. The observed toxicity profile of tarlatamab is considered acceptable in the context of a life-threatening disease.
L1784: Analysis of safety was based on the 90-day updated safety datasets using DCO of October 2, 2023. Missing adverse events from Study 20200491 were assessed and did not change the overall safety profile of tarlatamab. Refer to Section 8.2.4 (Treatment-emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions) for further information on unreported adverse events.
L1785: FDA carefully considered the need for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) given the major identified safety findings of CRS and neurologic toxicity including ICANS and have determined that a REMS is not needed; please refer to Section 12 for details. Based on our review, we have determined that the toxicity profile of tarlatamab can be adequately conveyed through labeling. In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks.
L1786: ## SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L1787: ## 8.3. Statistical Issues
L1788: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1789: There were no major statistical issues with this application. In the primary efficacy population (n=99), the confirmed ORR per BICR was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%), which was considered clinically meaningful, and the tumor responses generally appeared durable with a median DOR of 9.7 (95% CI: 6.9, NE) months and a median follow-up time of 15.1 months. The analyses of time-to-event endpoints such as PFS and OS are difficult to interpret in the absence of an appropriate comparator.
L1790: ## 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L1791: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1792: The review team recommends accelerated approval of tarlatamab under the provisions of 21 CFR 314.510 Subpart H for the treatment of adult patients with ES-SCLC who have progressed on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy.
L1793: The recommendation is based on the favorable benefit-risk assessment for tarlatamab
L1794: observed from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLLphi-301) and from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and Study 20160323 in ES-SCLC, a serious and life-threatening condition.
L1795: The primary efficacy population consisted of 99 patients enrolled in from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 who received tarlatamab at the proposed dose. Of the 99 patients, 73 patients (74%) had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy; of these 73 patients, 58 (59%) received anti-PD-(L)1 therapy in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in the first line setting. The confirmed ORR by BICR was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%), with a median DoR of 9.7 months (range 2.7 to 20.7+ months). Of the patients with responses, 68% of patients had an observed DoR \ge6 months and 40% of patients had an observed DoR \ge12 months. The observed ORR and DoR were clinically meaningful, and responses were observed across subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive and platinum-refractory disease and across subgroups with various tumor DLL3 expression cut-offs. Although the ORR point estimates differ between platinum sensitivity subgroups (31% in 42 patients with platinum sensitive SCLC and 52% in 27 patients with platinum resistant SCLC), the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in efficacy with tarlatamab based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely.
L1796: In context of approved therapies in the U.S. for the proposed indication, the ORR and DoR data from the primary efficacy population in Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated patient population (3rd line or greater) is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with topotecan, the only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with SCLC previously treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum sensitive disease and 2-7% in platinum resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in the primary efficacy population from Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I inhibitor as second line therapy. Of the remaining patients, the grand majority (89%) received second line therapies considered treatment options per U.S. standard of care. The ORR in the subgroups of patients who received prior topoisomerase I inhibitor, and who did not receive prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (with or without patients who received standard of care therapies), were generally consistent across subgroups and consistent with the ORR observed in the overall study population. Furthermore, it is not expected that treatment with additional therapies following platinum-based chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of response to tarlatamab relative to treatment with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy following platinum-based chemotherapy. In light of the observed results and particularly considering the ORR observed with tarlatamab in patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which far exceeds ORR reported with standard of care therapies, the available data for tarlatamab supports the proposed indication for treatment of SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
L1797: Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial, conducted as a PMR. The Applicant proposes to verify the clinical benefit of tarlatamab in the treatment of patients with SCLC who have disease
L1798: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L1799: progression on or after prior platinum-containing therapy, based on the results of an ongoing randomized controlled trial, DeLLphi-304 (Study 20210004), comparing tarlatamab to investigator's choice standard of care therapy with a primary endpoint of overall survival (OS).

L1828: ## 12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L1829: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1830: The Division of Oncology 2 (DO2) worked closely with the Division of Risk Management (DRM) and were aligned in the conclusion that a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) was not necessary to mitigate the risks of cytokine release syndrome (CRS) and neurologic toxicity including immune-effector cell associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS) associated with tarlatamab-dlle. The Applicant did not propose a REMS; however, the serious risks associated with tarlatamab warranted further review. Similar to other BiTE (bispecific T-cell engager) molecules, tarlatamab is associated with CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS.
L1831: ## Background
L1832: Imdelltra (tarlatamab-dlle), a new molecular entity submitted by Amgen Inc., is a bispecific delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3)-directed CD3 T-cell engager proposed for the treatment of adult patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy. Tarlatamab is administered as an intravenous (IV) infusion over one hour. The first full dose is administered following a step-up dose to reduce the incidence and severity of CRS. After the step-up dose, tarlatamab is administered every 2 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

L1843: ## Cytokine Release Syndrome
L1844: In the pooled safety population (n = 187), CRS occurred in 55% of patients, including 34% Grade 1, 19% Grade 2, 1.1% Grade 3 and 0.5% Grade 4 of tarlatamab-treated patients. Recurrent CRS occurred in 24% of tarlatamab-treated patients (18% Grade 1, 6% Grade 2).
L1845: Most events (43%) of CRS occurred after the first dose with 29% of patients experiencing any grade CRS after the second dose and 9% of patients experiencing CRS following the third dose or later. Following the Day 1, Day 8, Day 15 infusions, 16%, 4.3%, and 2.1% of patients experienced ≥ Grade 2 CRS, respectively. The median time to onset of all grade CRS from most recent dose of IMDELLTRA was 13.5 hours (range: 1 to 268 hours). The median time to onset of ≥ Grade 2 CRS from most recent dose of IMDELLTRA was 14.6 hours (range: 2 to 566 hours).
L1846: ## Neurologic Toxicity including ICANS
L1847: In the pooled safety population (n=187), neurologic toxicity including ICANS, occurred in 47% of patients who received tarlatamab, including 10% Grade 3. The most frequent neurologic toxicities were headache (14%), peripheral neuropathy (7%), dizziness (7%), insomnia (6%), muscular weakness (3.7%), delirium (2.1%), syncope (1.6%) and neurotoxicity (1.1%).
L1848: ICANS occurred in 9% of IMDELLTRA-treated patients. Recurrent ICANS occurred in 1.6% of patients. Most patients experienced ICANS following cycle 2 day 1 (24%). Following Day 1, Day 8, and Day 15 infusions, 0.5%, 0.5% and 3.7% of patients experienced ≥ Grade 2 ICANS, respectively. The median time to onset of ICANS from the first dose of IMDELLTRA was 29.5 days (range: 1 to 154 days). ICANS can occur several weeks following administration of IMDELLTRA. The median time to resolution of ICANS was 33 days (range: 1 to 93 days).
L1849: ## Rationale for not recommending a REMS
L1850: - If approved, labeling will be sufficient to mitigate the risks of CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS by including:A Boxed Warning for CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS
- Important Dosing Information (section 2.1 of the proposed label)
L1851: - IMDELLTRA should only be administered by a qualified healthcare professional with appropriate medical support to manage severe reactions such as CRS and neurologic toxicity including immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS) (similar to other approved products).
- Due to the risk of CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS, patients should be monitored from the start of the IMDELLTRA infusion for 22 to 24 hours on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 1 Day 8 in an appropriate healthcare settingRecommendations for monitoring in labeling that mirror the clinical trial protocol (refer to Table 1)
- Guidelines for Grading and Management of both CRS and ICANS (aligned with other approved drug products)
- The Applicant did not provide study investigators with any specific education and training for managing CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS outside of the standard clinical protocol training (an information request was sent requesting all training materials be submitted to the FDA).
- The rates of CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS are of low grade and similar to other BiTE drug products approved without a REMS.No fatal Grade 5 events
- CRS incidence = 55%
- Neurotoxicity incidence = 38%
- ICANS incidence = 9%
- Tocilizumab use = 9%
- Stakeholder/Oncologists InputAt a March 8, 2024, conference, the Associate Supervisory Director for DO2 spoke with academic oncologists specializing in the treatment of lung cancer regarding whether community oncologists would be expected to have familiarity with these serious risks. Anecdotally, the oncologists responded that community oncologists are likely to be familiar with these risks as they do not only treat patients with solid tumors, but they also treat patients with hematologic malignancies whose drug therapies are more likely to have the adverse effects of CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS.
- CAR-T REMS Changes
L1852: - On March 14, 2024, the Breyanzi (lisocabtagene maraleucel) REMS was modified to remove training requirements to minimize the burden on the healthcare delivery system of complying with the REMS. This modification is one product in the class modification of REMS for BCMA- or CD 19-directed genetically modified autologous T-cell immunotherapies. Part of the rationale for this class-wide modification includes the availability of non-REMS training materials developed by professional societies. These materials support greater awareness and education for the CAR T cell product class, including the recognition and management of toxicities. This highlights the burgeoning availability of literature, outside of a REMS, for the management of CRS and neurologic toxicity, including ICANS, for CAR T products and beyond.This objective was removed from the Breyanzi REMS goal: Ensuring that those who prescribe, dispense, or administer BREYANZI are aware of how to manage the risks of CRS and neurologic toxicities.
- The following REMS materials were removed: REMS Training Program and associated Knowledge Assessment.
L1853: DRM and DO2 agree, if tarlatamab is approved, a REMS is not necessary. The REMS Oversight Committee (ROC) was informed that the review team concluded a REMS is not necessary to ensure the benefits of tarlatamab outweighs its risks for the treatment of ES-SCLC. The ROC agreed with the review team's rationale.
L1854: ## 13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
L1855: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1856: The following Post-Marketing Requirements and Commitments were issued and agreed upon with the Applicant.
L1857: ## Postmarketing Requirements:
L1858: - Conduct a multicenter randomized clinical trial intended to verify and describe the clinical benefit of tarlatamab in patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) who have had disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
L1859: Final Protocol Submission: 12/2022 (Completed)
L1860: Trial Completion: April 2026
L1861: Final Report Submission: August 2026
L1862: ## PMR Rationale
L1863: Currently, ORR supported by DoR from the 20200491 single-arm trial is the primary basis for accelerated approval. The current available treatment (topotecan) was approved over 15 years ago in a platinum-sensitive SCLC population only. Time-to-event endpoints such as overall survival (OS) and progression-free survival (PFS) cannot be adequately interpreted in single-arm clinical trials due to confounding effects of heterogeneity of the patient population. Long term efficacy outcomes including OS from a randomized controlled clinical trial are needed to confirm clinical benefit of tarlatamab.
L1864: - Conduct an integrated safety analysis of data from patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer to further characterize the long-term incidence, severity, and outcome of the known serious risks of cytokine release syndrome, immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity. Include a comprehensive analysis from all available data sources including but not limited to patient-level and pooled analyses of ongoing and completed clinical trials. These data could also be obtained from the confirmatory study titled "A Randomized, Open-label, Phase 3 Study of Tarlatamab Compared With Standard of Care in Subjects With Relapsed Small Cell Lung Cancer After Platinum-based First-line Chemotherapy (DeLLphi-304)".
L1865: Draft Analysis Plan: January 2024
L1866: Final Analysis Plan: April 2025
L1867: Final Report Submission: August 2025
L1868: ## PMR Rationale
L1869: Tarlatamab is a half-life extended (HLE) bispecific T cell engager (BiTE®) that targets both delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) on target cells and CD3 on T cells. Adverse events of special interest for tarlatamab include cytokine release syndrome, immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity.
L1870: The safety is primarily supported by pooled data from patients in Studies 20200491 and 20160323 with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (SCLC) who received tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg. Patients received step up dose of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1, followed by target dose of 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8 and Cycle 1 Day 15 and every 2 weeks thereafter. Among 160 patients with extensive stage SCLC treated with the intended marketing dose of tarlatamab 10 mg in Studies 20200491 and 20160323, the incidences of Grade 3-4 adverse events were 64% and fatal adverse events were 2.4%. Serious adverse reactions occurred in 54% and adverse reactions led to dose interruptions occurred in 27% and discontinuation in 8%. The most common reasons for treatment delay (\ge 3\%) were fatigue and cytokine release syndrome. The safety data with tarlatamab is reflective of a median duration of exposure 14.4 weeks (range: 0.1 to 93.4) with 17% exposed for 6 months longer and 14% exposed for greater than 1 year. Further data is needed to identify and characterize safety with longer use of tarlatamab given the intended administration until progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. The goal of this PMR is to provide additional safety data to further characterize the adverse reactions of interest with longer-term use of tarlatamab, including, but not limited to, cytokine release syndrome, immune effector-cell associated neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity.
L1871: ## Postmarketing Commitment
L1872: - Conduct an integrated analysis from ongoing, completed, or planned clinical trials and other potential data sources as appropriate enrolling a sufficient representation of United States (U.S.) racial and ethnic minority patients that is reflective of the U.S. population of patients with SCLC, to further characterize the efficacy, safety and pharmacokinetics of tarlatamab in these patients. In the analysis, include a sufficient number of patients enrolled in the U.S., and a sufficient number of racial and ethnic minorities reflective of the incidence of SCLC in each subpopulation to allow for interpretation of the results. The analyses should support comparative efficacy and safety outcome analyses between the aforementioned populations and White patients.
L1873: Draft Analysis Plan: January 2025
L1874: Final Analysis Plan: April 2025
L1875: Final Report Submission: April 2027
L1876: PMC Rationale
L1877: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
## {Tarlatamab}
L1878: Study 20200491 was conducted globally and only 3% of the efficacy population in this application was enrolled in the U.S. In addition, racial and ethnic minorities reflective of the U.S. population of patients with SCLC, particularly Black/African American and Hispanic/Latino patients, were significantly underrepresented in the efficacy and safety population that supported this application, with no Black/African American patients and only one Hispanic/Latino patient enrolled. Therefore, it is important to evaluate data further characterizing the efficacy and safety of tarlatamab for the treatment of SCLC in study populations whose demographics are more representative of the population of U.S. patients for the proposed indication.

L1969: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1970: FDA reviewed the financial disclosures for all study investigators. Of the 689 investigators participating in Study 20200491 (as stated above by the Applicant), 679 investigators (99%) did not have significant financial disclosures to report; 2 investigators (0.3%) reported significant financial disclosures, while the Applicant did not have complete financial disclosure information on 8 investigators (1.2%).
L1971: Regarding the two investigators from the same site who reported significant financial disclosures, both indicated disclosable interest according to 21 CFR 54.2(f) (significant payments of other sorts means payments made by the Applicant of a covered study to the investigator or the institution to support activities of the investigator that have a monetary value of more than $25,000, exclusive of the costs of conducting the clinical study or other clinical studies, (e.g., a grant to fund ongoing research, compensation in the form of equipment or retainers for ongoing consultation or honoraria) during the time the clinical investigator is carrying out the study and for 1 year following the completion of the study). However, the payment amount did not reach the threshold indicated. [REDACTED] (b) (6) was enrolled in [REDACTED] (b) (6) of the study and this patient received the [REDACTED] (b) (6) mg dose of tarlatamab.
L1972: Of the 543 investigators participating in Study 20160323, 540 investigators (99%) did not have significant financial disclosures to report; no investigators reported significant financial disclosures, while the Applicant did not have complete financial disclosure information on 3 investigators (0.6%).
L1973: Based on the above, it is the opinion of the FDA clinical review team that the submitted financial disclosures as well as the absence of disclosures in a small number of investigators in Study 20200491 are very unlikely to have impacted the study in a meaningful way.

L1982: ## 19.4.1. Pharmacokinetic
L1983: Summaries of PK parameters following other dosing regimens of tarlatamab in study 20160323 are presented in the Table 62. The peak and trough serum concentrations for the 1/10 mg and 1/100 mg are summarized in Table 63.
L1984: Excluding dosage cohorts with only 1 evaluable patient (ranges from 0.003 to 0.1 mg), the dose-normalized AUC_{\tau} and C_{max} are generally consistent across the dosage groups, which suggests that tarlatamab exposure appears to be approximately dose proportional across the clinically relevant dosage range of 1 mg to 100 mg Q2W in study 20160323 (Table 62). Applicant did not report exposure for 200 mg Q2W due to limited data.

L1999: ## 19.4.2. Pharmacodynamic
L2000: ## Cytokine
L2001: Blood samples for cytokine analysis were collected on pre- and post-dose C1D1, C1D8, and C1D15. Cytokine elevation is observed following 1/10 mg of patients without tocilizumab use in
L2002: study 20160323. Excluding the single patient data, transient elevation of circulating cytokines was observed at dosages starting at 0.3 mg Q2W (Figure 26, Figure 27, Figure 28, Figure 29, and Figure 30). TNF-alpha did not increase significantly from baseline in studies 20160323 or 20200491. Of note, in cytokine profiles were similar in 1/10 mg and 1/100 mg.

L2024: ## T Cell Distribution
L2025: T-cell redistributions after first dose of tarlatamab infusion were characterized by in study 20160323 and 20200491 (Figure 31 and Figure 32). In Study 20160323, following initial infusion, peripheral CD8+CD69+ T-cell increased transiently 7.4% to 12.2% from baseline within 24 hours after infusion. Based on Study 20160323's result, there is no clear dose dependent effect in the T cell activation.
L2026: In study 20200491, both 1/10 and 1/100 mg increased the activation of CD8+CD69+T-cells, with 1/100 mg demonstrating a higher mean increase from baseline (27.8%), as compared to 1/10 mg (16.3%). However, the increase in T cell activation from 1/10 to 1/100 mg dosages did not translate to a clinically meaningful increase in response rate and this PD effect is not considered to be clinically relevant.
L2027: Figure 30. Median changes from pre-dose in percentage for CD69+ CD8+ T-cells activation in study 20160323.
L2028: The change from baseline for CD69+ CD8+ T-cells (%CD8) (y-axis) vs. visit time point (x-axis) for patients in cohorts who received target dose \le 3mg (red), 10mg (blue), 30mg (yellow), 100mg (green). The lines are the median changes from pre-dose (baseline) by timepoints across doses. The error bars indicate the median absolute deviation (MAD). The dots indicate the raw value of change from pre-dose (baseline) for each patient at each visit time point. C, treatment cycle; D, day of treatment cycle. Red dash line indicates the baseline level.

The lines are the mean changes from pre-dose (baseline) by timepoints across doses. The error bar indicates the 95% confidence interval (95% CI). The asterisks indicate the difference of Ismeans between 10mg and 100mg cohorts are significant at the visit time points per pairwise t test. C, treatment cycle; D, day of treatment cycle. Red dash line indicates the baseline level.
L2031: ## 19.4.3. Population PK Analysis
L2032: ## 19.4.3.1. Executive Summary
L2033: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2034: The PPK analysis supports the proposed dose of 1 mg on day 1, followed by 10 mg on day 8, 15 and every 2 weeks thereafter in patients with advanced SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy. No clinically relevant differences in exposure were identified according to patient characteristics, including age, bodyweight, sex, race (Asian and White), mild or moderate renal impairment, or mild hepatic impairment. The effect of severe renal impairment and moderate to severe hepatic impairment on the PK of tarlatamab are unknown due to data limitation.
L2035: Overall, the Applicant's PPK analyses for tarlatamab is considered acceptable for the intended analysis objectives and can reasonably well describe the observed concentration-time profile. The Applicant's analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. In addition, the developed model is deemed acceptable for deriving exposure metrics for E-R analysis and the model simulation results were acceptable to support the labeling statements regarding the general PK information and the effects of intrinsic and extrinsic factors on tarlatamab PK.
L2036: The simulated tarlatamab exposures following the Applicant's proposed retreatment plan after dose delay support their proposed repriming strategy, which is expected to effectively mitigate the incidence and severity of rebound CRS following restart of tarlatamab.
L2037: ## 19.4.3.2. PPK Assessment Summary
L2038: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2039: Based on cross-study population PK analyses:
L2040: - For a 73 kg typical white individual, tarlatamab clearance, volume of distribution of central compartment, volume of distribution of peripheral compartment, and volume of distribution at steady state were estimated to be 0.65 L/day, 3.44 L, 5.06 L, and 8.5 L, respectively. The inter-individual variability for tarlatamab clearance and volume of distribution of central compartment were 43.7% and 38.7%, respectively.
- Like other biologics, body weight was found to be correlated with tarlatamab clearance, with increase in body weight associated with increase in clearance. However, at the proposed clinical regimen of 1 mg on day 1 followed by 10 mg on day 8, day 15, and Q2W thereafter, body weight had no clinically relevant impact on tarlatamab efficacy as assessed by confirmed ORR or DCR (Figures 15 and 16 of Report 157687 [Exposure-response Analysis Report] [Module 5.3.4.2]).
- Positive ADA binding status was identified as a significant time-varying covariate on tarlatamab clearance. Individuals with positive ADA binding status at any time during the study were estimated to have higher clearance. Overall, the impact of ADA was not clinically relevant as displayed by subgroup analyses for efficacy endpoints in ADA positive and ADA negative subjects in Study 20200491 (Section 11.3.1 of Study 20200491 and Integrated Summary of Immunogenicity [Module 5.3.5.3]).
- Race was found to be correlated with volume of distribution of central compartment; however, race had a negligible impact on exposures.
L2041: Tarlatamab PK were not significantly impacted by other factors such as age, sex, ethnicity, serum albumin, number of prior lines of therapies, baseline disease burden or ECOG status, or renal or hepatic function.
L2042: General Information
Objectives of PPK Analysis	The objectives of the analysis were to characterize the population pharmacokinetics of tarlatamab in subjects with small cell lung cancer (SCLC), to evaluate the effects of demographic variables (covariates) on pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters, and to assess the impacts of identified covariates on tarlatamab exposures.
Study Included	20160323 and 20200491
Dose(s) Included	In these studies, tarlatamab was administered with short or extended intravenous infusion, and using no-step, one-step or two-step dosing regimens. Evaluated target doses included 0.003mg, 0.01mg, 0.03mg, 0.1mg, 0.3mg, 1mg, 3mg, 10mg, 30mg, and 100mg Q2W or 200mg Q3W (Table 2 and Table 5 of Amgen Pharmacometrics Report: 157688)
